## Module 7: Evaluation, Ethics, and Future of Generative AI

This module delves into the critical aspects of evaluating generative AI models, understanding their ethical implications, and exploring the exciting future directions of diffusion models. You will learn how to quantitatively and qualitatively assess the quality and diversity of generated images, identify and mitigate biases, navigate the complex ethical landscape of misinformation and copyright, and contribute to the responsible development of this transformative technology.

---

### Chapter 7.1 — Quantitative Metrics for Image Generation

#### Learning objectives
*   Understand the limitations of human perception in evaluating generative models and the necessity of quantitative metrics.
*   Explain the principles behind common image generation metrics like FID, IS, LPIPS, and CLIP Score.
*   Learn how to set up environments and use libraries to calculate these metrics for diffusion models.
*   Interpret the results of quantitative metrics to assess model performance in terms of realism and diversity.

#### Detailed lesson content
Evaluating the quality and diversity of images generated by diffusion models is a nuanced challenge. While human perception is the ultimate arbiter of aesthetic appeal and realism, it is subjective, slow, and prone to biases. What one person considers "realistic" or "creative" might differ significantly from another's opinion. Furthermore, manually sifting through thousands of generated images to assess diversity or identify subtle artifacts is impractical and inefficient for large-scale model development. This is where quantitative metrics become indispensable, providing objective, reproducible measures that allow us to compare different models, track progress during training, and identify areas for improvement. These metrics attempt to capture aspects of image quality and diversity that correlate with human judgment, albeit imperfectly.

One of the most widely used and respected metrics for generative models is the **Fréchet Inception Distance (FID)**. FID measures the "distance" between the distribution of features of real images and the distribution of features of generated images. It does this by feeding both sets of images through a pre-trained Inception-v3 network, extracting feature representations from an intermediate layer, and then modeling these features as multivariate Gaussian distributions. The FID score is then calculated as the Fréchet distance (also known as the Wasserstein-2 distance) between these two Gaussian distributions. A lower FID score indicates that the generated images are more similar to the real images in terms of their feature distribution, implying higher quality and realism. It's crucial to understand that FID requires a sufficiently large sample of both real and generated images (typically at least 10,000) to provide a stable and reliable estimate. A common mistake is to calculate FID on too few samples, leading to noisy and unrepresentative scores.

Another important metric is the **Inception Score (IS)**, though it's less frequently used for diffusion models compared to FID. IS aims to measure two aspects: the "cleanness" (quality) and "diversity" of generated images. It uses a pre-trained Inception-v3 network to classify generated images. For "cleanness," it expects the classifier to be confident about the object in each generated image (low entropy in class predictions for individual images). For "diversity," it expects a wide variety of objects across the entire set of generated images (high entropy in the marginal distribution of class predictions). A higher IS generally indicates better quality and diversity. However, IS has limitations; it only considers the classes known by the Inception network and doesn't compare generated images directly to a real dataset, making it less robust for assessing realism than FID.

The **Learned Perceptual Image Patch Similarity (LPIPS)** metric, often referred to as "perceptual distance," addresses a common issue with traditional pixel-wise metrics like Mean Squared Error (MSE): they don't align well with human perception. Two images might be pixel-wise very different but perceptually identical (e.g., a slight shift or rotation). LPIPS, on the other hand, uses features extracted from a pre-trained deep neural network (like AlexNet, VGG, or SqueezeNet) to compare image patches. It measures the weighted L2 distance between the feature stacks of two images, where the weights are learned. When used for generative models, LPIPS can quantify the diversity of generated samples by comparing pairs of generated images, or it can be used to measure the perceptual similarity between a generated image and its corresponding real image in tasks like image-to-image translation. A lower LPIPS score indicates higher perceptual similarity.

Finally, the **CLIP Score** has emerged as a powerful metric, especially for text-to-image diffusion models. CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training) is a multimodal model trained to understand the relationship between images and text. The CLIP Score measures the semantic similarity between a generated image and the text prompt used to generate it. It does this by embedding both the image and the text prompt into a shared latent space using the CLIP model and then calculating the cosine similarity between their embeddings. A higher CLIP Score indicates that the generated image aligns better with the semantic content of the input text prompt. This is particularly useful for evaluating the "controllability" and "faithfulness" of text-conditional generation. For example, if you prompt "a cat riding a skateboard," a high CLIP score suggests the generated image indeed depicts a cat on a skateboard, not just a cat or a skateboard separately.

Calculating these metrics typically involves using specialized libraries. For instance, `torchmetrics` provides implementations for FID and IS, while `lpips` is a dedicated library for LPIPS. For CLIP Score, you'd directly use the Hugging Face `transformers` library to load a pre-trained CLIP model. When implementing, ensure your image preprocessing (resizing, normalization) matches what the pre-trained feature extractor (e.g., Inception-v3 for FID/IS, CLIP model for CLIP Score) expects. Incorrect preprocessing is a common pitfall that can lead to erroneous metric values. Always use consistent image sizes and normalization ranges. For example, Inception-v3 expects images to be resized to 299x299 pixels and normalized to the range [-1, 1].

```python
import torch
from torchvision.transforms import ToTensor, Normalize, Resize, Compose
from torchvision.datasets import CIFAR10
from torch.utils.data import DataLoader
from tqdm import tqdm
from torchmetrics.image.fid import FrechetInceptionDistance
from torchmetrics.image.inception import InceptionScore
from PIL import Image
import numpy as np
import lpips
from transformers import CLIPProcessor, CLIPModel

# --- Configuration ---
NUM_SAMPLES = 1000 # For demonstration, use a larger number like 10000+ for real evaluation
BATCH_SIZE = 64
IMAGE_SIZE = 299 # Required for InceptionV3 (FID/IS)

# --- 1. Data Loading and Preprocessing ---
# Dummy generated images (replace with your actual diffusion model output)
def generate_dummy_images(num_images, img_size):
    # In a real scenario, this would be your diffusion model generating images
    # For demonstration, we create random noise images
    return [Image.fromarray((np.random.rand(img_size, img_size, 3) * 255).astype(np.uint8)) for _ in range(num_images)]

# Load real images (e.g., from CIFAR-10 for demonstration)
cifar_dataset = CIFAR10(root='./data', train=True, download=True)
real_images_pil = [cifar_dataset[i][0] for i in range(NUM_SAMPLES)]

# Generate dummy images for comparison
generated_images_pil = generate_dummy_images(NUM_SAMPLES, IMAGE_SIZE)

# Preprocessing for InceptionV3 (FID/IS)
# InceptionV3 expects images in range [0, 255] as uint8 or normalized to [-1, 1]
# torchmetrics FID/IS expects uint8 images in range [0, 255]
transform_inception = Compose([
    Resize(IMAGE_SIZE),
    ToTensor(), # Converts to [0, 1] float
    lambda x: (x * 255).to(torch.uint8) # Converts to [0, 255] uint8
])

real_images_inception = torch.stack([transform_inception(img) for img in real_images_pil])
generated_images_inception = torch.stack([transform_inception(img) for img in generated_images_pil])

# --- 2. Calculate FID ---
print("\nCalculating FID...")
fid = FrechetInceptionDistance(feature=2048, normalize=True) # normalize=True means input is [0, 255] uint8
fid.update(real_images_inception, real=True)
fid.update(generated_images_inception, real=False)
fid_score = fid.compute()
print(f"FID Score: {fid_score.item():.2f}")

# --- 3. Calculate Inception Score (IS) ---
print("\nCalculating Inception Score...")
# IS also expects uint8 images in range [0, 255]
inception = InceptionScore(normalize=True, splits=10) # splits for variance calculation
inception.update(generated_images_inception)
is_mean, is_std = inception.compute()
print(f"Inception Score: Mean={is_mean.item():.2f}, Std={is_std.item():.2f}")

# --- 4. Calculate LPIPS (Perceptual Loss) ---
print("\nCalculating LPIPS...")
# LPIPS expects images in range [-1, 1]
transform_lpips = Compose([
    Resize(64), # LPIPS often works with smaller images, or scale down
    ToTensor(), # Converts to [0, 1] float
    Normalize(mean=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5], std=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5]) # Converts to [-1, 1]
])

# For LPIPS, we typically compare generated images to a reference, or generated to generated for diversity
# Here, let's compare a subset of generated images to a subset of real images
# In a real scenario, you might compare generated images to their ground truth if it's an image-to-image task,
# or compute average LPIPS between randomly selected pairs of generated images for diversity.
lpips_model = lpips.LPIPS(net='alex').eval() # Using AlexNet as the backbone

real_images_lpips = torch.stack([transform_lpips(img) for img in real_images_pil[:BATCH_SIZE]])
generated_images_lpips = torch.stack([transform_lpips(img) for img in generated_images_pil[:BATCH_SIZE]])

# Calculate LPIPS between corresponding real and generated images (for a subset)
lpips_scores = []
for i in range(BATCH_SIZE):
    score = lpips_model(real_images_lpips[i].unsqueeze(0), generated_images_lpips[i].unsqueeze(0))
    lpips_scores.append(score.item())

avg_lpips = np.mean(lpips_scores)
print(f"Average LPIPS (Real vs. Generated, {BATCH_SIZE} samples): {avg_lpips:.4f}")

# --- 5. Calculate CLIP Score ---
print("\nCalculating CLIP Score...")
# CLIP expects images to be preprocessed by its own processor
clip_model = CLIPModel.from_pretrained("openai/clip-vit-base-patch32")
clip_processor = CLIPProcessor.from_pretrained("openai/clip-vit-base-patch32")

# Example text prompts (replace with actual prompts used for generation)
text_prompts = ["a photo of a dog", "a beautiful landscape", "an abstract painting", "a cat riding a skateboard"]
# For demonstration, let's assume the first 4 generated images correspond to these prompts
generated_images_clip_pil = generated_images_pil[:len(text_prompts)]

clip_scores = []
for i, (image_pil, prompt) in enumerate(zip(generated_images_clip_pil, text_prompts)):
    inputs = clip_processor(text=prompt, images=image_pil, return_tensors="pt", padding=True)
    with torch.no_grad():
        outputs = clip_model(**inputs)
    
    # Cosine similarity between image_embeds and text_embeds
    image_embeds = outputs.image_embeds
    text_embeds = outputs.text_embeds
    
    # Normalize embeddings for cosine similarity
    image_embeds = image_embeds / image_embeds.norm(p=2, dim=-1, keepdim=True)
    text_embeds = text_embeds / text_embeds.norm(p=2, dim=-1, keepdim=True)
    
    similarity = (image_embeds * text_embeds).sum(dim=-1).item()
    clip_scores.append(similarity)
    print(f"CLIP Score for prompt '{prompt}': {similarity:.4f}")

avg_clip_score = np.mean(clip_scores)
print(f"Average CLIP Score ({len(text_prompts)} samples): {avg_clip_score:.4f}")
```

Safety Note: When using pre-trained models for feature extraction (like Inception-v3 or CLIP), ensure you download them from trusted sources (e.g., PyTorch Hub, Hugging Face) to avoid malicious code injection. Also, be mindful of the computational resources required for these metrics, especially FID, which can be memory-intensive for large datasets.

#### Key concepts
*   **Fréchet Inception Distance (FID):** A metric that quantifies the similarity between the feature distributions of real and generated images using a pre-trained Inception-v3 network. Lower FID indicates higher realism and quality.
*   **Inception Score (IS):** A metric that measures the "cleanness" (quality) and "diversity" of generated images based on the predictions of a pre-trained Inception-v3 classifier. Higher IS indicates better quality and diversity.
*   **Learned Perceptual Image Patch Similarity (LPIPS):** A perceptual metric that uses deep features from pre-trained networks to measure the similarity between images, aligning better with human perception than pixel-wise metrics. Lower LPIPS indicates higher perceptual similarity.
*   **CLIP Score:** A metric that evaluates the semantic alignment between a generated image and its corresponding text prompt, using the embeddings from a Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) model. Higher CLIP Score indicates better text-image consistency.
*   **Feature Distribution:** The statistical pattern of how features (e.g., from a deep neural network) are spread across a dataset of images. FID compares these distributions.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Evaluate your fine-tuned Stable Diffusion model with FID and CLIP Score**

In a previous module, you fine-tuned a Stable Diffusion model. Now, you will use quantitative metrics to evaluate its performance.

1.  **Generate a dataset of images:** Use your fine-tuned Stable Diffusion model to generate at least 1000 images using a diverse set of prompts. Save these images to a directory (e.g., `generated_images/`).
2.  **Prepare a real image dataset:** Select a corresponding real image dataset (e.g., a subset of LAION-5B, or a domain-specific dataset if you fine-tuned on one). Ensure you have at least 1000 images and save them to a directory (e.g., `real_images/`).
3.  **Implement FID calculation:** Use the `torchmetrics` library to calculate the FID score between your generated images and the real images.
4.  **Implement CLIP Score calculation:** For a subset of your generated images (e.g., 100-200 images), calculate the CLIP Score against the prompts you used to generate them.
5.  **Analyze results:** Discuss what your FID and CLIP scores tell you about the quality, diversity, and text-alignment of your fine-tuned model. How do these scores compare to a base Stable Diffusion model (if you have a reference)?

```python
import torch
from torchvision.transforms import ToTensor, Resize, Compose
from torch.utils.data import Dataset, DataLoader
from PIL import Image
import os
import numpy as np
from tqdm import tqdm
from torchmetrics.image.fid import FrechetInceptionDistance
from transformers import CLIPProcessor, CLIPModel

# --- Configuration ---
NUM_EVAL_SAMPLES = 2000 # Use a larger number for robust evaluation, e.g., 10000+
BATCH_SIZE = 64
FID_IMAGE_SIZE = 299 # InceptionV3 input size
CLIP_MODEL_NAME = "openai/clip-vit-base-patch32"

GENERATED_IMAGES_DIR = "./your_generated_images/" # Path to your generated images
REAL_IMAGES_DIR = "./your_real_images/"         # Path to your real reference images
PROMPT_FILE = "./your_prompts.txt"              # File containing prompts used for generation

# --- Helper Dataset Class ---
class ImageFolderDataset(Dataset):
    def __init__(self, folder_path, transform=None):
        self.folder_path = folder_path
        self.image_filenames = [os.path.join(folder_path, f) for f in os.listdir(folder_path) if f.endswith(('.png', '.jpg', '.jpeg'))]
        self.transform = transform

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.image_filenames)

    def __getitem__(self, idx):
        img_path = self.image_filenames[idx]
        image = Image.open(img_path).convert("RGB")
        if self.transform:
            image = self.transform(image)
        return image

# --- Image Preprocessing ---
# For FID: InceptionV3 expects uint8 images in range [0, 255]
transform_fid = Compose([
    Resize(FID_IMAGE_SIZE),
    ToTensor(), # Converts to [0, 1] float
    lambda x: (x * 255).to(torch.uint8) # Converts to [0, 255] uint8
])

# For CLIP: CLIP processor handles its own specific preprocessing
# We'll pass PIL images directly to clip_processor

# --- Main Evaluation Function ---
def run_evaluation(generated_dir, real_dir, prompt_file, num_samples):
    device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
    print(f"Using device: {device}")

    # --- Load Datasets ---
    generated_dataset = ImageFolderDataset(generated_dir, transform=transform_fid)
    real_dataset = ImageFolderDataset(real_dir, transform=transform_fid)

    # Limit samples for evaluation if requested
    generated_samples = min(len(generated_dataset), num_samples)
    real_samples = min(len(real_dataset), num_samples)

    generated_dataloader_fid = DataLoader(torch.utils.data.Subset(generated_dataset, range(generated_samples)), batch_size=BATCH_SIZE, shuffle=False, num_workers=4)
    real_dataloader_fid = DataLoader(torch.utils.data.Subset(real_dataset, range(real_samples)), batch_size=BATCH_SIZE, shuffle=False, num_workers=4)

    # --- 1. Calculate FID ---
    print("\n--- Calculating FID ---")
    fid_metric = FrechetInceptionDistance(feature=2048, normalize=True).to(device)

    print("Updating FID with real images...")
    for batch in tqdm(real_dataloader_fid, desc="Real Images"):
        fid_metric.update(batch.to(device), real=True)

    print("Updating FID with generated images...")
    for batch in tqdm(generated_dataloader_fid, desc="Generated Images"):
        fid_metric.update(batch.to(device), real=False)

    fid_score = fid_metric.compute()
    print(f"Final FID Score: {fid_score.item():.2f}")

    # --- 2. Calculate CLIP Score ---
    print("\n--- Calculating CLIP Score ---")
    clip_model = CLIPModel.from_pretrained(CLIP_MODEL_NAME).to(device)
    clip_processor = CLIPProcessor.from_pretrained(CLIP_MODEL_NAME)

    prompts = []
    if os.path.exists(prompt_file):
        with open(prompt_file, 'r') as f:
            prompts = [line.strip() for line in f if line.strip()]
    else:
        print(f"Warning: Prompt file '{prompt_file}' not found. CLIP Score will use dummy prompts.")
        prompts = [f"a generated image {i}" for i in range(generated_samples)]

    # Ensure we have enough prompts for generated images, or vice versa
    num_clip_eval = min(generated_samples, len(prompts))
    print(f"Evaluating CLIP Score for {num_clip_eval} samples.")

    generated_dataset_pil = ImageFolderDataset(generated_dir) # Load PIL images for CLIP
    clip_scores = []

    for i in tqdm(range(num_clip_eval), desc="CLIP Score"):
        image_pil = generated_dataset_pil[i]
        prompt = prompts[i]

        inputs = clip_processor(text=prompt, images=image_pil, return_tensors="pt", padding=True).to(device)
        with torch.no_grad():
            outputs = clip_model(**inputs)

        image_embeds = outputs.image_embeds
        text_embeds = outputs.text_embeds

        image_embeds = image_embeds / image_embeds.norm(p=2, dim=-1, keepdim=True)
        text_embeds = text_embeds / text_embeds.norm(p=2, dim=-1, keepdim=True)

        similarity = (image_embeds * text_embeds).sum(dim=-1).item()
        clip_scores.append(similarity)

    if clip_scores:
        avg_clip_score = np.mean(clip_scores)
        print(f"Average CLIP Score: {avg_clip_score:.4f}")
    else:
        print("No CLIP scores calculated.")

# Example usage:
# Make sure to replace GENERATED_IMAGES_DIR, REAL_IMAGES_DIR, and PROMPT_FILE
# with your actual paths before running.
# Example:
# os.makedirs(GENERATED_IMAGES_DIR, exist_ok=True)
# os.makedirs(REAL_IMAGES_DIR, exist_ok=True)
# # Populate these directories with your generated and real images
# # Create a dummy prompt file if you don't have one
# with open(PROMPT_FILE, 'w') as f:
#     for i in range(NUM_EVAL_SAMPLES):
#         f.write(f"a high-quality image of a subject {i}\n")

# run_evaluation(GENERATED_IMAGES_DIR, REAL_IMAGES_DIR, PROMPT_FILE, NUM_EVAL_SAMPLES)
```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You have trained a new diffusion model and obtained an FID score of 15.0. An older, state-of-the-art model achieved an FID of 12.5 on the same dataset. What can you conclude about your new model's performance based solely on this metric, and what are some potential reasons for the difference?
    *   **Correct Answer:** A lower FID score indicates better performance in terms of realism and diversity, as it signifies that the generated image distribution is closer to the real image distribution. Therefore, the older, state-of-the-art model (FID 12.5) is performing better than your new model (FID 15.0) according to this metric. Potential reasons for the difference could include:
        *   **Training data:** Your model might have been trained on a smaller, less diverse, or lower-quality dataset.
        *   **Model architecture:** The older model might have a more optimized or advanced architecture.
        *   **Training hyperparameters:** Suboptimal learning rate, number of training steps, or other hyperparameters in your training.
        *   **Sampling strategy:** The older model might use a more advanced sampling method (e.g., DPM-Solver, DDIM with specific steps) that produces higher quality images.
        *   **Mode collapse:** Your model might be suffering from some degree of mode collapse, generating less diverse images, which would increase FID.

2.  **Question:** Explain the primary advantage of using the CLIP Score for evaluating text-to-image diffusion models compared to FID or IS. What kind of model failure would CLIP Score be particularly good at detecting?
    *   **Correct Answer:** The primary advantage of the CLIP Score is its ability to directly measure the **semantic alignment or faithfulness between the generated image and the input text prompt**. While FID and IS assess overall image quality and diversity relative to a real dataset (FID) or internal consistency (IS), they do not directly evaluate if the generated image accurately represents the *specific content described by the text prompt*. A model failure that CLIP Score would be particularly good at detecting is **prompt misalignment or hallucination**. For example, if a model generates a beautiful landscape when prompted for "a futuristic cityscape," its FID might still be good if the landscapes are realistic, but its CLIP Score for that specific prompt would be very low, indicating a failure to follow the text instruction. It effectively measures the "controllability" of the text-to-image generation process.

### Chapter 7.2 — Qualitative Evaluation and Human Perception Studies

#### Learning objectives
*   Recognize the inherent limitations of quantitative metrics and the indispensable role of human judgment in evaluating generative AI.
*   Design and conduct basic human perception studies (e.g., A/B tests, Likert scales) to gather subjective feedback on generated images.
*   Identify common biases and pitfalls in human evaluation processes and strategies to mitigate them.
*   Interpret qualitative feedback to gain insights into model strengths, weaknesses, and areas for improvement beyond what metrics can capture.

#### Detailed lesson content
While quantitative metrics like FID and CLIP Score provide objective, reproducible measures of image quality and faithfulness, they often fall short in capturing the full spectrum of human aesthetic appreciation, creativity, and the subtle nuances of realism. An image might achieve an excellent FID score, indicating statistical similarity to real images, yet still appear "uncanny" or lack the artistic flair that humans value. Conversely, a highly creative or stylized image might have a poor FID if it deviates significantly from the real image distribution, even if it's considered high-quality by humans. This disconnect highlights the indispensable role of **qualitative evaluation** and **human perception studies** in assessing generative AI. Human evaluators can identify subtle artifacts, assess subjective qualities like "beauty," "creativity," "emotional impact," or "coherence" that are difficult for algorithms to quantify. They can also provide invaluable feedback on how well a model meets specific user needs or artistic intentions.

Designing effective human perception studies requires careful consideration to minimize bias and ensure reliable results. One common approach is **A/B testing**, where participants are shown two images (A and B) and asked to choose which one they prefer based on a specific criterion (e.g., "Which image looks more realistic?", "Which image is more creative?", "Which image better matches the prompt?"). The images could be from two different models, or one could be real and the other generated. To ensure fairness, the order of A and B should be randomized, and participants should not be informed which image comes from which source. Another popular method involves using **Likert scales**, where participants rate images on a scale (e.g., 1 to 5, where 1 is "Strongly Disagree" and 5 is "Strongly Agree") for various attributes like realism, diversity, aesthetic appeal, or prompt adherence. For example, "Rate the realism of this image" or "Rate how well this image matches the text description." This provides more granular feedback than a simple A/B choice.

When setting up a human evaluation, it's crucial to define clear evaluation criteria. Without specific instructions, evaluators might use their own subjective biases, leading to inconsistent results. For instance, if you're evaluating "realism," provide examples of what constitutes a realistic image and what doesn't. If you're evaluating "creativity," explain what you mean by creative within the context of your task. The selection of evaluators is also critical. For general aesthetic tasks, a diverse group of laypersons might be sufficient. For domain-specific applications (e.g., medical imaging, architectural design), expert evaluators would be necessary. Always aim for a sufficiently large and diverse pool of evaluators to ensure statistical significance and representativeness of your findings. A common mistake is using too few evaluators or a homogenous group, which can lead to biased or ungeneralizable results.

Common pitfalls in human evaluation include **evaluator fatigue**, where participants become less attentive or consistent over time, and **priming effects**, where prior questions or images influence subsequent judgments. To mitigate fatigue, keep evaluation sessions reasonably short, incorporate breaks, and randomize the order of images presented. To avoid priming, ensure that the presentation order of images and questions is thoroughly shuffled for each participant. Another significant challenge is **selection bias** in the images chosen for evaluation. If you only show the "best" generated images, your results will be artificially inflated. It's important to sample images randomly from your generated dataset or use a stratified sampling approach to ensure representative coverage of different generation qualities and styles.

Analyzing qualitative data involves more than just averaging scores. While mean Likert scores provide a summary, it's equally important to look at the distribution of responses, identify outliers, and analyze free-text comments. Free-text feedback can reveal specific types of artifacts, unexpected interpretations of prompts, or novel strengths that quantitative metrics completely miss. For instance, a comment like "The hands look distorted" or "The lighting is inconsistent with the scene" provides actionable insights for model developers. Tools for survey creation like Google Forms, SurveyMonkey, or specialized platforms like Mechanical Turk or Prolific Academic can facilitate data collection for human evaluation studies. When collecting data, always prioritize **ethical considerations**: ensure informed consent, protect participant privacy, and anonymize data where possible. Be transparent about the purpose of the study and how the data will be used.

Consider a practical scenario: you've fine-tuned a Stable Diffusion model to generate images of specific types of furniture. You use FID and CLIP Score to assess its overall quality and prompt adherence. However, your design team reports that while the images look realistic, they often contain subtle structural inconsistencies or proportions that make them unusable for product mockups. This is a perfect case for qualitative evaluation. You would set up a human perception study where professional designers rate generated furniture images on attributes like "structural integrity," "realistic proportions," and "suitability for product visualization" using a Likert scale. You might also include an open-ended comment box for specific feedback. This human input would then guide your next steps in fine-tuning, perhaps by focusing on architectural consistency or training with more geometrically precise datasets.

```python
import random
from IPython.display import display, HTML, Image as IPImage
from PIL import Image
import os
import io
import base64

# --- Dummy Data Setup (replace with your actual image paths) ---
# In a real scenario, you would have actual image files from your model
# and potentially real images for comparison.
# For this example, we'll simulate image paths.

# Create dummy image files for demonstration
dummy_image_dir = "./dummy_evaluation_images"
os.makedirs(dummy_image_dir, exist_ok=True)

def create_dummy_image(filename, text="Dummy Image"):
    img = Image.new('RGB', (256, 256), color = (random.randint(0,255), random.randint(0,255), random.randint(0,255)))
    from PIL import ImageDraw, ImageFont
    d = ImageDraw.Draw(img)
    try:
        fnt = ImageFont.truetype("arial.ttf", 20)
    except IOError:
        fnt = ImageFont.load_default()
    d.text((10,10), text, fill=(255,255,255), font=fnt)
    img.save(os.path.join(dummy_image_dir, filename))

for i in range(5):
    create_dummy_image(f"model_A_{i}.png", f"Model A Image {i}")
    create_dummy_image(f"model_B_{i}.png", f"Model B Image {i}")
    create_dummy_image(f"real_{i}.png", f"Real Image {i}")

# --- Simulated Image Paths and Prompts ---
# In a real scenario, these would be actual paths to your generated images
# and the prompts used to create them.
images_model_A = [f"model_A_{i}.png" for i in range(5)]
images_model_B = [f"model_B_{i}.png" for i in range(5)]
real_images = [f"real_{i}.png" for i in range(5)]

prompts = [
    "A majestic cat sitting on a cloud.",
    "A futuristic city at sunset.",
    "An ancient warrior in a dense forest.",
    "A serene lake with mountains in the background.",
    "A robot playing chess with a human."
]

# --- Helper function to display images in Jupyter/Colab ---
def display_image_from_path(image_path, width=200):
    with open(os.path.join(dummy_image_dir, image_path), "rb") as f:
        encoded_string = base64.b64encode(f.read()).decode()
    return f'<img src="data:image/png;base64,{encoded_string}" width="{width}px" style="margin: 5px; border: 1px solid #ddd;">'

# --- Human Evaluation Framework ---

def run_ab_test(prompt, img_path_A, img_path_B, evaluation_criterion="which image is more realistic"):
    print(f"\n--- A/B Test for Prompt: '{prompt}' ---")
    print(f"Criterion: {evaluation_criterion}")

    # Randomize A and B presentation
    if random.random() < 0.5:
        display_A = display_image_from_path(img_path_A)
        display_B = display_image_from_path(img_path_B)
        actual_A_label = "Model A"
        actual_B_label = "Model B"
    else:
        display_A = display_image_from_path(img_path_B)
        display_B = display_image_from_path(img_path_A)
        actual_A_label = "Model B"
        actual_B_label = "Model A"

    html_content = f"""
    <div style="display: flex; justify-content: center; align-items: flex-start; margin-bottom: 20px;">
        <div style="text-align: center; margin-right: 20px;">
            <h3>Image 1</h3>
            {display_A}
        </div>
        <div style="text-align: center;">
            <h3>Image 2</h3>
            {display_B}
        </div>
    </div>
    <p>Based on the prompt: "<b>{prompt}</b>", and the criterion: "<b>{evaluation_criterion}</b>", which image do you prefer?</p>
    <form>
        <input type="radio" name="choice" value="1"> Image 1<br>
        <input type="radio" name="choice" value="2"> Image 2<br>
        <input type="radio" name="choice" value="neither"> Neither / Both are equally good/bad<br>
        <label for="feedback">Optional feedback:</label><br>
        <textarea id="feedback" name="feedback" rows="2" cols="50"></textarea><br>
        <input type="submit" value="Submit">
    </form>
    """
    display(HTML(html_content))
    # In a real system, you'd capture this input via a backend.
    # For this demo, imagine the user makes a choice.
    print(f"  (Imagine user chose: Image 1, which was {actual_A_label})")

def run_likert_scale_evaluation(prompt, img_path, evaluation_criteria):
    print(f"\n--- Likert Scale Evaluation for Prompt: '{prompt}' ---")
    display(HTML(display_image_from_path(img_path, width=300)))

    html_content = f"""
    <p>Based on the prompt: "<b>{prompt}</b>", please rate the image below on the following criteria:</p>
    <form>
    """
    for criterion in evaluation_criteria:
        html_content += f"""
        <p><b>{criterion}</b></p>
        <input type="radio" name="{criterion.replace(' ', '_')}" value="1"> 1 (Strongly Disagree/Very Poor)<br>
        <input type="radio" name="{criterion.replace(' ', '_')}" value="2"> 2<br>
        <input type="radio" name="{criterion.replace(' ', '_')}" value="3"> 3 (Neutral)<br>
        <input type="radio" name="{criterion.replace(' ', '_')}" value="4"> 4<br>
        <input type="radio" name="{criterion.replace(' ', '_')}" value="5"> 5 (Strongly Agree/Excellent)<br>
        """
    html_content += """
        <label for="feedback">Optional general feedback:</label><br>
        <textarea id="feedback" name="feedback" rows="2" cols="50"></textarea><br>
        <input type="submit" value="Submit">
    </form>
    """
    display(HTML(html_content))
    # In a real system, you'd capture this input via a backend.
    # For this demo, imagine the user makes choices.
    print(f"  (Imagine user rated 'Realism': 4, 'Prompt Adherence': 5, etc.)")

# --- Example Usage ---
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print("--- Starting Human Perception Study Simulation ---")

    # Example A/B Test: Compare Model A vs Model B for realism
    for i in range(len(prompts)):
        run_ab_test(prompts[i], images_model_A[i], images_model_B[i], "which image looks more realistic?")

    # Example Likert Scale Test: Evaluate Model A for specific attributes
    likert_criteria = ["The image is realistic.", "The image matches the prompt well.", "The image is aesthetically pleasing.", "The image shows good diversity of elements."]
    for i in range(len(prompts)):
        run_likert_scale_evaluation(prompts[i], images_model_A[i], likert_criteria)

    print("\n--- Human Perception Study Simulation Complete ---")
    print("Remember to clean up dummy images if no longer needed: os.remove(path_to_image)")

```

Safety Note: When conducting human studies, always adhere to ethical guidelines, including obtaining informed consent, ensuring data privacy, and avoiding any potentially harmful or offensive content. If using crowdsourcing platforms, be aware of their terms of service and ethical guidelines.

#### Key concepts
*   **Qualitative Evaluation:** Subjective assessment of generative model outputs by human observers, focusing on aspects like aesthetics, creativity, coherence, and subtle artifacts.
*   **Human Perception Studies:** Structured experiments designed to gather human feedback on generated content, often involving A/B tests or Likert scales.
*   **A/B Testing:** A method where participants choose between two options (A and B) based on a specific criterion, often used to compare two models or a model vs. real.
*   **Likert Scale:** A psychometric scale commonly used in surveys, where respondents rate their agreement or satisfaction on a symmetric scale (e.g., 1-5).
*   **Evaluator Fatigue:** A phenomenon where the quality or consistency of human judgments degrades over prolonged evaluation sessions.
*   **Priming Effect:** When exposure to one stimulus influences the response to a subsequent stimulus, potentially biasing evaluation results.
*   **Selection Bias:** The error introduced when the images chosen for evaluation are not representative of the overall model output, leading to skewed results.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Design a Human Evaluation Study for Prompt Adherence**

Imagine you have fine-tuned a Stable Diffusion model to generate images of specific animals in various poses and environments. You want to assess how well the model adheres to complex prompts like "A golden retriever playing fetch in a snowy forest at sunset."

1.  **Define Evaluation Criteria:** List 3-5 specific criteria that human evaluators should consider when judging "prompt adherence." Think about aspects like "Does the image contain all specified objects?", "Are the objects in the correct context/environment?", "Is the pose accurate?", "Is the lighting/time of day correct?".
2.  **Choose Evaluation Method:** Decide whether an A/B test (comparing your model's output to a base model's output for the same prompt) or a Likert scale (rating your model's output on each criterion) would be more appropriate for this specific goal. Justify your choice.
3.  **Outline Study Protocol:**
    *   How many images would you show per participant?
    *   How would you select the images (e.g., random sampling, specific challenging prompts)?
    *   What instructions would you give to evaluators?
    *   How would you mitigate evaluator fatigue and bias?
    *   What kind of demographic information (if any) would you collect from evaluators?
4.  **Create a Sample Survey Question:** Write out one A/B test question or one Likert scale question with its corresponding options, as if it were part of your survey.

```markdown
**1. Defined Evaluation Criteria for Prompt Adherence:**
*   **Object Presence & Accuracy:** Are all specified objects (e.g., "golden retriever", "snowy forest") clearly present and correctly depicted?
*   **Context & Environment Consistency:** Does the background/environment accurately reflect the prompt (e.g., "snowy forest")?
*   **Action/Pose Fidelity:** Is the described action or pose (e.g., "playing fetch") accurately represented?
*   **Attribute Adherence:** Are specific attributes (e.g., "golden" for retriever, "sunset" for lighting) correctly applied?
*   **Overall Coherence:** Does the entire image logically cohere with the prompt's description, without contradictory elements?

**2. Chosen Evaluation Method and Justification:**
I would choose a **Likert scale evaluation** for this specific goal.
*   **Justification:** While an A/B test could compare my fine-tuned model against a base model, a Likert scale allows for a more granular assessment of *how well* a single image adheres to *multiple specific aspects* of a complex prompt. This provides richer, diagnostic feedback beyond just "better" or "worse." For prompt adherence, it's not just about which image is generally better, but *where* the model succeeds or fails in following specific instructions. This helps pinpoint exact areas for further fine-tuning (e.g., "model struggles with specific lighting conditions").

**3. Outline Study Protocol:**
*   **Number of Images per Participant:** 20-30 images. This is a manageable number to prevent significant evaluator fatigue while still providing a decent sample size for individual feedback.
*   **Image Selection:**
    *   Randomly sample 15-20 challenging prompts from a diverse set (including edge cases or prompts with multiple interacting elements).
    *   For each of these prompts, generate 1-2 images from the fine-tuned model.
    *   Include 5-10 "control" images (e.g., from a base model or known good/bad examples) to gauge evaluator consistency.
*   **Evaluator Instructions:**
    *   Clearly state the purpose: "Evaluate how well the generated image matches the provided text prompt."
    *   Provide a detailed explanation of each Likert scale criterion with examples of what constitutes a "1" (poor) vs. a "5" (excellent) for each.
    *   Emphasize focusing *only* on prompt adherence, not general aesthetic appeal (unless aesthetic appeal is part of prompt adherence, e.g., "a beautiful sunset").
    *   Instruct evaluators to provide free-text comments for any significant observations or discrepancies.
*   **Mitigation of Fatigue and Bias:**
    *   **Randomization:** Randomize the order of images and criteria for each participant.
    *   **Breaks:** Suggest a short break after every 10 images.
    *   **Clear Definitions:** Provide unambiguous definitions for all criteria.
    *   **Diverse Evaluator Pool:** Recruit evaluators from diverse backgrounds (e.g., via a crowdsourcing platform like Prolific) to minimize demographic bias.
    *   **Anonymity:** Ensure all responses are anonymous.
*   **Demographic Information:** Collect basic, non-identifying demographic data like age range, gender (optional), and self-reported familiarity with AI/image generation (to segment results if needed).

**4. Sample Survey Question (Likert Scale):**

**Prompt:** "A golden retriever playing fetch in a snowy forest at sunset."

**Image:** (Display the generated image here)

Please rate the image on the following criteria (1 = Strongly Disagree, 5 = Strongly Agree):

*   **The image accurately depicts a golden retriever.**
    *   ○ 1 ○ 2 ○ 3 ○ 4 ○ 5
*   **The image shows the golden retriever playing fetch.**
    *   ○ 1 ○ 2 ○ 3 ○ 4 ○ 5
*   **The environment is clearly a snowy forest.**
    *   ○ 1 ○ 2 ○ 3 ○ 4 ○ 5
*   **The lighting in the image reflects a sunset.**
    *   ○ 1 ○ 2 ○ 3 ○ 4 ○ 5
*   **Overall, the image adheres well to the provided text prompt.**
    *   ○ 1 ○ 2 ○ 3 ○ 4 ○ 5

**Additional Comments (optional):**
[Text area for free-form feedback]
```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** A researcher is evaluating a new diffusion model for generating realistic portraits. They observe that while the FID score is excellent, human evaluators consistently rate the generated images as "uncanny" or "lacking emotional depth." Explain why this discrepancy might occur and what steps the researcher should take next.
    *   **Correct Answer:** This discrepancy highlights the limitations of quantitative metrics like FID. FID measures statistical similarity between feature distributions but doesn't fully capture subjective human perceptions of realism, emotional expression, or the "uncanny valley" effect. An excellent FID score suggests the images are statistically similar to real portraits but might miss subtle cues that humans use to distinguish true realism or emotional authenticity. The researcher should:
        *   **Deepen Qualitative Evaluation:** Conduct more focused human perception studies using Likert scales or open-ended feedback to pinpoint *why* images are perceived as uncanny (e.g., unnatural eyes, distorted facial expressions, inconsistent lighting).
        *   **Refine Metrics:** Explore other metrics that might be more sensitive to specific aspects of human faces, or develop custom metrics if possible.
        *   **Iterate on Model/Data:** Use the qualitative feedback to guide further model training or data curation. This might involve:
            *   Adding more diverse and high-quality training data for faces and expressions.
            *   Implementing specific architectural improvements or loss functions to enhance facial realism or emotional consistency.
            *   Fine-tuning with specific datasets focused on human expressions.

2.  **Question:** You are tasked with comparing two diffusion models (Model X and Model Y) for their ability to generate diverse images of animals. You decide to conduct an A/B test. Describe how you would set up this A/B test, including how you would present images to participants and what specific question you would ask to assess diversity.
    *   **Correct Answer:** To set up an A/B test for diversity:
        *   **Image Generation:** For a set of generic prompts (e.g., "a dog," "a cat," "a bird"), generate a *batch* of images from Model X and another batch from Model Y. It's crucial to generate multiple images per prompt to assess the *range* of outputs, not just a single best one.
        *   **Presentation:** For each prompt, select two *sets* of images (e.g., 5 images from Model X and 5 images from Model Y). Present these two sets side-by-side to the participant. To avoid bias, randomize which model's output appears on the left/right for each prompt. Crucially, do not label which set comes from which model.
        *   **Question:** The question should directly address diversity. Instead of asking "Which image is better?", ask: "Considering these two sets of images generated from the same prompt, which set demonstrates a *greater variety* in terms of pose, breed, background, or style?" or "Which model appears to generate a *more diverse range* of outputs for this animal?"
        *   **Data Collection:** Record the participant's choice for each prompt. Aggregate choices across many participants and prompts to determine which model is perceived as more diverse. Free-text feedback could also be collected to understand *how* evaluators perceive diversity (e.g., "Model X has more different breeds," "Model Y always generates the same pose").

### Chapter 7.3 — Bias and Fairness in Generative Models

#### Learning objectives
*   Identify various sources of bias in generative AI models, particularly diffusion models, stemming from training data and model design.
*   Analyze how biases manifest in generated images, leading to stereotypes, underrepresentation, or misrepresentation.
*   Explore quantitative and qualitative methods for detecting and measuring bias in image generation.
*   Learn practical strategies and techniques for mitigating bias in diffusion models, including data augmentation and debiasing methods.

#### Detailed lesson content
The promise of generative AI is to create novel and diverse content, but this promise is often undermined by the pervasive issue of bias. Generative models, especially diffusion models, are not inherently neutral; they learn from the vast datasets they are trained on, and these datasets inevitably reflect the biases present in the real world and in their collection processes. If a training dataset overrepresents certain demographics, stereotypes, or cultural norms, the generative model will learn and amplify these patterns, leading to outputs that are unfair, discriminatory, or simply unrepresentative of the true diversity of the world. Understanding the sources of bias and how to address them is a critical responsibility for anyone developing or deploying generative AI.

The primary source of bias in diffusion models is the **training data**. Large-scale image-text datasets like LAION-5B, while incredibly powerful, are scraped from the internet and thus inherit all the societal biases embedded in web content. This can manifest in several ways:
1.  **Demographic Bias:** Overrepresentation of certain racial groups, genders, or ages, leading to generated images that default to these groups. For example, prompting "a doctor" might predominantly generate images of male doctors, or "a CEO" might generate images of white men, simply because these were more prevalent in the training data.
2.  **Stereotypical Bias:** Associating certain professions, activities, or attributes with specific demographics. For instance, "a nurse" might generate female images, while "an engineer" generates male images.
3.  **Underrepresentation:** Entire groups or cultures might be underrepresented or completely absent from the training data, leading to a model that cannot generate images related to them or generates them poorly.
4.  **Quality Bias:** Images of certain subjects or demographics might be of lower quality, resolution, or artistic style in the training data, leading to lower quality generations for those subjects.
5.  **Geographical/Cultural Bias:** Datasets often heavily lean towards Western cultures, leading to models that struggle to generate diverse cultural scenes, clothing, or architecture accurately.

How do these biases manifest in generated images? They can lead to **stereotypical outputs**, where a prompt like "a person" consistently produces a young, white, conventionally attractive individual. They can result in **underrepresentation**, where attempts to generate images of specific minority groups yield poor quality or irrelevant results. They can also lead to **misrepresentation**, where certain groups are depicted in demeaning or inaccurate ways. Beyond societal biases, there can also be **technical biases** arising from model architecture or training procedures, though data bias is generally the more dominant factor for generative content. For example, certain sampling methods might inadvertently favor specific types of outputs.

Detecting and measuring bias requires both quantitative and qualitative approaches.
**Quantitative methods** often involve:
*   **Demographic Attribute Classification:** Using pre-trained classifiers (e.g., for gender, race, age) to analyze the demographics of generated images for a neutral prompt (e.g., "a person"). Comparing these distributions to real-world demographics or desired distributions can reveal bias.
*   **Prompt-based Bias Analysis:** Generating images for "neutral" prompts paired with specific attributes (e.g., "a doctor, male" vs. "a doctor, female") and then analyzing the quality, realism, or even the number of successful generations for each.
*   **Measuring Disparity in CLIP Scores:** If a model is biased, the CLIP score for a prompt like "a female engineer" might be consistently lower than for "a male engineer," indicating the model struggles more with the former.
*   **Attribute-specific FID/IS:** Calculating FID or IS for subsets of generated images corresponding to different demographic groups to see if quality differs.

**Qualitative methods** are equally important:
*   **Human Audits:** Expert human evaluators (potentially from diverse backgrounds) review generated images for stereotypes, misrepresentation, and underrepresentation. This is crucial for catching subtle biases that automated classifiers might miss.
*   **Community Feedback:** Engaging with affected communities to understand how they perceive the model's outputs and identify harmful biases.

Mitigating bias in diffusion models is an active area of research and requires a multi-faceted approach:
1.  **Data Curation and Augmentation:** This is often the most impactful strategy.
    *   **Balanced Datasets:** Actively seek out and include more diverse and representative data during dataset creation or fine-tuning. This might involve augmenting underrepresented groups.
    *   **Data Debiasing:** Techniques like re-sampling (oversampling minority groups, undersampling majority groups) or re-weighting (assigning higher loss weights to samples from underrepresented groups) can balance the dataset's influence during training.
    *   **Synthetic Data Generation:** Carefully generating synthetic data for underrepresented groups, though this must be done with caution to avoid amplifying existing biases or creating new ones.
2.  **Prompt Engineering and Debiasing at Inference:**
    *   **Neutral Prompts:** Encourage users to use neutral prompts when possible.
    *   **Affirmative Prompts:** Explicitly include diverse attributes in prompts (e.g., "a doctor of color," "a female CEO") to counteract default biases.
    *   **Inference-time Debiasing:** Research is exploring methods to adjust the diffusion process at inference time to steer generations away from biased outcomes. This could involve modifying the noise schedule or applying conditional guidance that promotes diversity.
3.  **Model Architecture and Training:**
    *   **Fairness-aware Loss Functions:** Incorporating loss terms that penalize biased outputs or promote diversity.
    *   **Disentangled Representations:** Designing models that learn disentangled representations of identity, attributes, and concepts, making it easier to control specific aspects without affecting others.
4.  **Transparency and Documentation:**
    *   **Model Cards and Data Cards:** Documenting the training data sources, known biases, and limitations of the model. This helps users understand potential biases and use the model responsibly.
    *   **Bias Audits:** Regularly auditing models for bias as part of their lifecycle.

A common mistake is to assume that simply training on a "large enough" dataset will eliminate bias. The internet, while vast, is not unbiased. Another pitfall is to apply debiasing techniques without careful consideration, potentially leading to unintended side effects or new biases. For example, over-correcting for one type of bias might introduce another. Always evaluate the impact of debiasing strategies comprehensively.

```python
import torch
from diffusers import StableDiffusionPipeline
from PIL import Image
import os
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from collections import Counter
import re

# --- Configuration ---
MODEL_ID = "runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5" # Using a common SD model for demonstration
NUM_GENERATIONS_PER_PROMPT = 10
OUTPUT_DIR = "./bias_analysis_generations"
os.makedirs(OUTPUT_DIR, exist_ok=True)

# --- Load Model ---
# Ensure you have logged in to Hugging Face if using private models or if required
# from huggingface_hub import login
# login()
pipe = StableDiffusionPipeline.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID, torch_dtype=torch.float16)
pipe = pipe.to("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")

# --- 1. Bias Detection: Prompt-based Analysis ---
# We'll use a set of neutral and gender-specific prompts for professions
professions = ["doctor", "engineer", "nurse", "CEO", "teacher", "software developer"]
genders = ["male", "female"]

prompts_to_evaluate = []
for profession in professions:
    prompts_to_evaluate.append(f"A photo of a {profession}") # Neutral
    for gender in genders:
        prompts_to_evaluate.append(f"A photo of a {gender} {profession}") # Gender-specific

print("--- Generating images for bias analysis ---")
generated_images_metadata = [] # To store prompt and generated image path

for prompt in prompts_to_evaluate:
    print(f"Generating for: '{prompt}'")
    for i in range(NUM_GENERATIONS_PER_PROMPT):
        try:
            image = pipe(prompt).images[0]
            filename = f"{re.sub(r'[^a-zA-Z0-9_]', '_', prompt)}_{i}.png"
            filepath = os.path.join(OUTPUT_DIR, filename)
            image.save(filepath)
            generated_images_metadata.append({"prompt": prompt, "filepath": filepath})
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error generating for '{prompt}': {e}")

print(f"\nGenerated {len(generated_images_metadata)} images in {OUTPUT_DIR}")

# --- 2. Simple Qualitative Analysis (Manual Review) ---
# In a real scenario, you would manually review these images or use a human evaluation platform.
# For this demo, we'll just print instructions.
print("\n--- Qualitative Bias Analysis (Manual Review) ---")
print("Please manually review the images in the './bias_analysis_generations/' directory.")
print("For each prompt (e.g., 'A photo of a doctor'), observe:")
print("  - What is the predominant gender/race/age of the generated individuals?")
print("  - Do the 'neutral' prompts lean towards a specific demographic?")
print("  - Are there any stereotypical depictions (e.g., 'nurse' always female, 'engineer' always male)?")
print("  - Is the quality of images consistent across different demographic prompts?")
print("  - Are there any offensive or inappropriate generations?")

# --- 3. (Optional) Quantitative Analysis with a simple Gender Classifier (Conceptual) ---
# This part is conceptual as training/integrating a robust gender/race classifier is complex.
# For a real project, you'd use a pre-trained face attribute classifier (e.g., from FairFace, DeepFace).
def simple_gender_classifier_mock(image_path):
    # This is a MOCK function. In reality, you'd load a pre-trained model.
    # For demonstration, we'll randomly assign genders with a bias.
    # Let's simulate a bias towards male for "engineer" and female for "nurse"
    filename = os.path.basename(image_path)
    if "engineer" in filename and random.random() < 0.8: # 80% male for engineer
        return "male"
    if "nurse" in filename and random.random() < 0.8: # 80% female for nurse
        return "female"
    return random.choice(["male", "female", "unclear"]) # Otherwise, random

print("\n--- Quantitative Bias Analysis (Conceptual Gender Classification) ---")
gender_counts_by_prompt = {}

for item in generated_images_metadata:
    prompt = item["prompt"]
    image_path = item["filepath"]
    
    # Skip gender-specific prompts for this analysis, focus on neutral ones
    if "male" in prompt or "female" in prompt:
        continue

    predicted_gender = simple_gender_classifier_mock(image_path) # Use your real classifier here
    
    if prompt not in gender_counts_by_prompt:
        gender_counts_by_prompt[prompt] = Counter()
    gender_counts_by_prompt[prompt][predicted_gender] += 1

print("\nSimulated Gender Distribution for Neutral Profession Prompts:")
for prompt, counts in gender_counts_by_prompt.items():
    total = sum(counts.values())
    if total > 0:
        print(f"Prompt: '{prompt}'")
        for gender, count in counts.items():
            print(f"  {gender}: {count} ({count/total:.1%})")
    else:
        print(f"Prompt: '{prompt}': No images classified.")

# --- 4. Mitigation Strategy (Conceptual: Data Augmentation Idea) ---
print("\n--- Conceptual Mitigation Strategy: Data Augmentation ---")
print("To mitigate observed biases (e.g., 'doctor' defaulting to male), consider:")
print("1. **Collecting more diverse data:** Specifically seek out images of female doctors, doctors of various ethnicities, etc.")
print("2. **Prompt-based augmentation:** During fine-tuning, for prompts like 'a doctor', also include augmented prompts like 'a female doctor', 'a male doctor', 'a doctor of color', to explicitly teach diversity.")
print("3. **Re-weighting:** If fine-tuning, assign higher weights to samples representing underrepresented groups in the loss function.")
print("\nExample of prompt augmentation during fine-tuning:")
print("Original prompt: 'a photo of a doctor'")
print("Augmented prompts: 'a photo of a female doctor', 'a photo of a male doctor', 'a photo of an Asian doctor', 'a photo of a Black doctor', 'a photo of an elderly doctor'")
print("By training with these explicit variations, the model learns to associate 'doctor' with a wider range of attributes.")

# Clean up dummy images
# import shutil
# shutil.rmtree(OUTPUT_DIR)
```

Safety Note: When performing bias analysis, be extremely careful with the interpretation of results, especially if using automated classifiers for sensitive attributes like race or gender. These classifiers themselves can be biased and may misclassify individuals, leading to erroneous conclusions. Always cross-reference with human audits and community feedback. Avoid perpetuating stereotypes even in the analysis phase.

#### Key concepts
*   **Bias in AI:** Systematic errors or unfairness in AI model outputs that reflect societal prejudices or imbalances in training data.
*   **Training Data Bias:** The primary source of bias in generative models, where the datasets used for training disproportionately represent certain demographics, stereotypes, or cultural norms.
*   **Demographic Bias:** Overrepresentation or underrepresentation of specific racial groups, genders, ages, or other demographic categories in generated content.
*   **Stereotypical Bias:** The tendency of a model to associate certain roles, attributes, or activities with specific demographic groups based on training data patterns.
*   **Underrepresentation:** The failure of a model to generate diverse or high-quality content for certain groups due to their scarcity in the training data.
*   **Prompt-based Bias Analysis:** A method for detecting bias by generating images for neutral prompts and prompts with explicit demographic attributes, then analyzing the outputs.
*   **Data Curation and Augmentation:** Strategies to mitigate bias by actively balancing training datasets, oversampling underrepresented groups, or generating synthetic diverse data.
*   **Inference-time Debiasing:** Techniques to modify the generation process during sampling to steer outputs away from biased outcomes.
*   **Model Cards/Data Cards:** Documentation practices that transparently disclose a model's training data, known biases, and limitations.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Analyze Bias in a Pre-trained Stable Diffusion Model**

You will conduct a mini-bias audit of a pre-trained Stable Diffusion model (e.g., `runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5`) for gender and racial bias in professional contexts.

1.  **Select Professions:** Choose 3-5 professions (e.g., "scientist", "chef", "construction worker", "artist").
2.  **Generate Images for Neutral Prompts:** For each profession, generate 10-15 images using a neutral prompt like "A photo of a [profession]". Save these images to a structured directory (e.g., `bias_audit/scientist/image_01.png`).
3.  **Generate Images for Gender-Specific Prompts:** For each profession, also generate 5 images for "A photo of a male [profession]" and 5 images for "A photo of a female [profession]". Save these similarly.
4.  **Qualitative Analysis:** Manually review the generated images for the neutral prompts.
    *   What is the predominant gender and apparent race/ethnicity for each profession?
    *   Are there any stereotypical depictions (e.g., clothing, setting)?
    *   Compare the quality of images generated for gender-specific prompts. Does the model struggle more with "female construction worker" than "male construction worker"?
5.  **Summarize Findings:** Write a short report (2-3 paragraphs) detailing your observations, highlighting any clear biases you found and speculating on their potential sources.

```python
import torch
from diffusers import StableDiffusionPipeline
from PIL import Image
import os
import re
from tqdm import tqdm

# --- Configuration ---
MODEL_ID = "runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5" # Or any other Stable Diffusion model
NUM_NEUTRAL_GENERATIONS = 15
NUM_GENDER_SPECIFIC_GENERATIONS = 5
OUTPUT_BASE_DIR = "./bias_audit_results"
os.makedirs(OUTPUT_BASE_DIR, exist_ok=True)

# --- Load Model ---
pipe = StableDiffusionPipeline.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID, torch_dtype=torch.float16)
pipe = pipe.to("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")

# --- Professions to Audit ---
professions_to_audit = ["scientist", "chef", "construction worker", "artist", "CEO"]

print(f"Starting bias audit for model: {MODEL_ID}")

# --- Step 2 & 3: Generate Images ---
for profession in professions_to_audit:
    print(f"\nGenerating images for profession: '{profession}'")

    # Neutral Prompts
    neutral_prompt = f"A photo of a {profession}"
    prof_neutral_dir = os.path.join(OUTPUT_BASE_DIR, profession, "neutral")
    os.makedirs(prof_neutral_dir, exist_ok=True)
    print(f"  Neutral prompt: '{neutral_prompt}'")
    for i in tqdm(range(NUM_NEUTRAL_GENERATIONS), desc=f"    Generating neutral for {profession}"):
        image = pipe(neutral_prompt).images[0]
        image.save(os.path.join(prof_neutral_dir, f"image_{i:02d}.png"))

    # Gender-Specific Prompts
    for gender in ["male", "female"]:
        gender_prompt = f"A photo of a {gender} {profession}"
        prof_gender_dir = os.path.join(OUTPUT_BASE_DIR, profession, gender)
        os.makedirs(prof_gender_dir, exist_ok=True)
        print(f"  Gender-specific prompt: '{gender_prompt}'")
        for i in tqdm(range(NUM_GENDER_SPECIFIC_GENERATIONS), desc=f"    Generating {gender} for {profession}"):
            image = pipe(gender_prompt).images[0]
            image.save(os.path.join(prof_gender_dir, f"image_{i:02d}.png"))

print(f"\nImage generation complete. Images saved to: {OUTPUT_BASE_DIR}")
print("Please proceed with manual qualitative analysis.")

# --- Step 4 & 5: Qualitative Analysis and Summary (Manual) ---
# This part is to be done manually by the learner after image generation.
# Example structure for the summary:

'''
**Bias Audit Report for Stable Diffusion Model (`runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5`)**

**Date:** [Current Date]
**Auditor:** [Your Name]

**1. Observations for Neutral Prompts:**

*   **Scientist:** When prompted with "A photo of a scientist", the model predominantly generated images of [e.g., white males in lab coats, often older]. There was [e.g., very little racial diversity, some gender diversity but still skewed male]. Stereotypes included [e.g., always in a lab, holding beakers].
*   **Chef:** For "A photo of a chef", the outputs were mostly [e.g., male, often with a white chef's hat, some racial diversity but still leaning Western]. Female chefs were [e.g., less common, or depicted in more domestic settings].
*   **Construction Worker:** "A photo of a construction worker" almost exclusively produced [e.g., muscular white males in hard hats and safety vests]. Racial and gender diversity was [e.g., almost non-existent].
*   **Artist:** "A photo of an artist" showed [e.g., more gender diversity, but still predominantly white individuals, often young, in bohemian settings]. Some racial diversity was present but not extensive.
*   **CEO:** "A photo of a CEO" consistently generated images of [e.g., white males in suits, often in corporate offices, appearing middle-aged or older]. Female or non-white CEOs were [e.g., extremely rare or absent].

**2. Observations for Gender-Specific Prompts:**

*   **General Quality:** For most professions, the model seemed to generate images of comparable quality for both "male [profession]" and "female [profession]". However, for some professions like "female construction worker" or "female CEO", the images sometimes felt less "natural" or less common than their male counterparts, or occasionally contained subtle artifacts.
*   **Stereotype Reinforcement:** Even with gender specified, stereotypes persisted. For example, "female nurse" often showed more nurturing, softer poses, while "male nurse" might show more authoritative or less emotional poses. "Female construction worker" sometimes depicted individuals in less active roles or with less robust gear compared to males.

**3. Potential Sources of Bias:**

The observed biases strongly suggest a **training data bias**. The large datasets used to train Stable Diffusion (like LAION-5B) are scraped from the internet, which inherently reflects existing societal biases and historical representation. Professions like "CEO" and "construction worker" have historically been male-dominated in many cultures, and this imbalance is reflected in the vast amount of online imagery. Similarly, "nurse" is often stereotypically associated with females. The model simply learns and reproduces these statistical patterns from its training distribution. While the model can generate diverse images when explicitly prompted (e.g., "female CEO"), its "default" or neutral generations reveal the underlying biases of its training data.
'''
```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You observe that your diffusion model, when prompted with "a scientist," predominantly generates images of older white men in lab coats. However, when you explicitly prompt "a young female scientist of color," it generates high-quality, diverse images. What does this tell you about the nature of the bias in your model, and what is a practical, data-centric mitigation strategy you could employ if you were fine-tuning this model?
    *   **Correct Answer:** This observation indicates that the model possesses a **demographic and stereotypical bias** in its default behavior, likely inherited from its training data. The model has learned that "scientist" is most commonly associated with older white men. However, the fact that it *can* generate high-quality, diverse images when explicitly prompted suggests that the underlying capabilities for diverse generation exist within the model's latent space; it's just not the default.
        A practical, data-centric mitigation strategy for fine-tuning would be **data augmentation with explicit attribute conditioning**. For example, when fine-tuning, for every image of a scientist (or any profession), you would generate multiple text prompts that explicitly describe the gender, age, and race/ethnicity of the individual in the image. You would also create synthetic prompts like "a young female scientist of color" and pair them with existing diverse images (or carefully curated synthetic ones) to reinforce these associations. This teaches the model to associate the general term "scientist" with a broader range of attributes, making diverse generations more probable even with neutral prompts.

2.  **Question:** Explain the concept of "underrepresentation" bias in generative AI and how it differs from "stereotypical bias." Provide an example of each in the context of image generation.
    *   **Correct Answer:**
        *   **Underrepresentation Bias:** This occurs when certain groups, objects, or concepts are either completely absent or significantly scarce in the training data. As a result, the generative model struggles to produce images related to these underrepresented entities, or the images it does produce are of lower quality, less diverse, or inaccurate.
            *   **Example:** If a diffusion model is trained primarily on images of Western architecture, it might fail to generate any coherent or realistic images when prompted with "traditional Maasai village" or "Japanese temple garden" because these concepts were underrepresented in its training data.
        *   **Stereotypical Bias:** This occurs when the model associates specific attributes, roles, or activities with particular demographic groups or objects, based on their disproportionate co-occurrence in the training data. The model learns and reinforces these societal stereotypes.
            *   **Example:** If a diffusion model is prompted with "a nurse," and it consistently generates images of young women in traditional nurse uniforms, even if the prompt was neutral on gender or age, this demonstrates stereotypical bias. It's not that the model *can't* generate male nurses, but its default output for "nurse" is heavily skewed by the stereotype present in its training data.
