## Final Capstone Project

The capstone project is your opportunity to synthesize the knowledge and skills gained throughout this course. You will choose one of three project options, each designed to challenge you to apply diffusion models in a practical, creative, or analytical way. These projects require integrating concepts from multiple modules, including model understanding, prompt engineering, fine-tuning, and evaluation.

### Project Option 1: Fine-tuning a Stable Diffusion Model for a Niche Aesthetic or Object Generation

This project challenges you to specialize a pre-trained Stable Diffusion model to generate images in a very specific style or of a particular type of object that the base model might struggle with. This involves curating a dataset, performing fine-tuning, and demonstrating the model's enhanced capabilities through targeted prompt engineering.

*   **Requirements:**
    *   **Dataset Curation:** Assemble a high-quality dataset of at least 50-100 images representative of your chosen niche (e.g., "vintage sci-fi book covers," "hyperrealistic miniature food," "abstract geometric patterns"). Ensure images are appropriately sized and captioned.
    *   **Model Fine-tuning:** Select a pre-trained Stable Diffusion model (e.g., `runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5` or `stabilityai/stable-diffusion-2-1`) and fine-tune it using techniques like LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) or DreamBooth. Document your training parameters (learning rate, epochs, batch size, etc.).
    *   **Prompt Engineering & Generation:** Generate at least 10 distinct images using your fine-tuned model, demonstrating its ability to produce high-quality, on-topic content. Include both simple and complex prompts, showcasing variations and specific details.
    *   **Evaluation & Analysis:** Present a qualitative analysis of your model's performance. Compare generated images from your fine-tuned model against those from the base model using identical prompts. Discuss improvements, limitations, and potential biases.
    *   **Code & Report:** Submit all code (Jupyter Notebooks preferred), the curated dataset (or instructions to reproduce it), generated images, and a brief report (2-3 pages) detailing your methodology, findings, and reflections.

*   **Stretch Goals:**
    *   Implement a custom loss function or regularization technique during fine-tuning.
    *   Explore different fine-tuning methods beyond LoRA/DreamBooth.
    *   Build a simple Gradio or Streamlit interface to interact with your fine-tuned model.
    *   Conduct a small user study (e.g., with friends/family) to gather feedback on the generated images.

*   **Evaluation Criteria:**
    *   **Dataset Quality & Relevance (20%):** How well does the dataset represent the chosen niche? Is it clean and well-prepared?
    *   **Fine-tuning Execution (30%):** Correct application of fine-tuning techniques, appropriate parameter selection, and clear documentation of the process.
    *   **Generated Image Quality & Diversity (30%):** Aesthetic appeal, adherence to the niche, and variety of outputs from different prompts.
    *   **Analysis & Comparison (20%):** Insightful discussion of results, clear comparison between base and fine-tuned models, and identification of strengths/weaknesses.

*   **Estimated Time:** 20-30 hours

### Project Option 2: Interactive Image-to-Image Transformation with Conditional Diffusion

This project involves creating a system that takes an input image (e.g., a sketch, a segmentation map, or a low-resolution photo) and transforms it into a new image based on a textual prompt and the conditional input. This often leverages models like ControlNet or similar conditioning mechanisms to guide the generation process.

*   **Requirements:**
    *   **Choose a Conditional Input:** Select a type of conditional input (e.g., Canny edge map, depth map, segmentation mask, pose estimation, or a simple sketch).
    *   **Model Integration:** Utilize a pre-trained ControlNet model (or a similar conditional diffusion model) with a base Stable Diffusion model. Demonstrate how to load and use these models effectively.
    *   **Input Processing:** Develop code to process an input image into the required conditional format (e.g., generating a Canny edge map from a photograph, or allowing users to draw a simple sketch).
    *   **Interactive Generation:** Implement a script or a simple web interface (e.g., using Gradio or Streamlit) that allows a user to:
        1.  Upload or draw an input image.
        2.  Provide a text prompt.
        3.  Generate a new image based on the conditional input and prompt.
    *   **Demonstration & Analysis:** Provide examples of input images, their corresponding conditional maps, the prompts used, and the generated outputs. Discuss the effectiveness of the conditioning, the impact of different prompts, and any challenges encountered.
    *   **Code & Report:** Submit all code, a set of example inputs/outputs, and a brief report (2-3 pages) explaining your approach, the chosen conditional type, and observations.

*   **Stretch Goals:**
    *   Allow for multiple conditional inputs (e.g., Canny + Depth).
    *   Implement an in-painting or out-painting feature using a mask.
    *   Explore real-time generation or optimize the inference speed.
    *   Add controls for parameters like guidance scale, number of inference steps, or negative prompts in your interface.

*   **Evaluation Criteria:**
    *   **Conditional Input Processing (25%):** Accuracy and robustness of generating the conditional map from the input.
    *   **Model Integration & Functionality (30%):** Correct use of ControlNet/conditional model, smooth integration with the base model.
    *   **Interactive Experience (25%):** Usability and clarity of the interface (if built), ease of generating diverse outputs.
    *   **Demonstration & Analysis (20%):** Quality of generated examples, insightful discussion of conditional guidance effectiveness and limitations.

*   **Estimated Time:** 25-35 hours

### Project Option 3: Exploring Latent Space Interpolation and Semantic Editing with Diffusion Models

This project focuses on understanding and manipulating the latent space of diffusion models to achieve smooth transitions between images or perform semantic edits. You will investigate how changes in the latent representation correspond to changes in the generated image, enabling creative applications like animation or style transfer.

*   **Requirements:**
    *   **Latent Space Exploration:** Choose two distinct prompts or two distinct generated images. Generate their corresponding latent representations (if applicable, or derive them through inversion).
    *   **Interpolation Implementation:** Implement a method to interpolate between these two latent vectors. This could be simple linear interpolation, spherical linear interpolation (slerp), or a more advanced path in the latent space.
    *   **Image Sequence Generation:** Generate a sequence of at least 15-20 images by sampling along the interpolated latent path. This sequence should visually demonstrate a smooth transition between the initial and final states.
    *   **Semantic Editing (Optional but Recommended):** Choose a specific semantic attribute (e.g., "add glasses," "change season to winter," "make it futuristic") and attempt to edit an existing image or a generated image by manipulating its latent representation or by using prompt weighting/blending techniques.
    *   **Analysis & Visualization:** Present the generated image sequence (e.g., as a GIF or video). Analyze the smoothness of the interpolation and discuss how semantic attributes change. For semantic editing, show before-and-after comparisons and explain your methodology.
    *   **Code & Report:** Submit all code, the generated image sequence/GIF, and a report (3-4 pages) detailing your interpolation method, observations about the latent space, and the effectiveness of any semantic editing attempts.

*   **Stretch Goals:**
    *   Implement more sophisticated latent pathfinding algorithms (e.g., using PCA or t-SNE on multiple latents).
    *   Combine interpolation with prompt engineering to guide the transition more effectively.
    *   Develop a tool for "latent space arithmetic" (e.g., `image_A + attribute_B - attribute_C`).
    *   Explore how different sampling schedulers affect the interpolation quality.

*   **Evaluation Criteria:**
    *   **Latent Space Understanding (25%):** Clear demonstration of how latent vectors are obtained and manipulated.
    *   **Interpolation Quality (30%):** Smoothness and visual coherence of the generated image sequence.
    *   **Semantic Editing Effectiveness (25%):** (If attempted) Success in achieving desired semantic changes, clear methodology.
    *   **Analysis & Visualization (20%):** Insightful discussion of latent space properties, clear presentation of results (GIF/video).

*   **Estimated Time:** 20-30 hours

## Final Examination

This examination assesses your comprehensive understanding of generative AI, specifically focusing on diffusion models for image generation. It covers theoretical concepts, practical implementation details, and problem-solving scenarios encountered when working with these powerful models.

---

**Instructions:** Answer all questions thoroughly. Show your work for coding and design problems.

### Section 1: Concept Definitions (4 Questions)

1.  **Question:** Explain the core concept of the "diffusion process" in Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPMs). Describe the forward (noising) process and the reverse (denoising) process, highlighting their mathematical relationship and purpose.
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:** The diffusion process in DDPMs involves two main stages:
        *   **Forward (Noising) Process:** This is a fixed Markov chain that gradually adds Gaussian noise to an input image over a series of `T` timesteps. Starting with a clean image $x_0$, at each timestep $t$, a small amount of Gaussian noise is added to produce $x_t$. This process is designed such that for a sufficiently large `T`, the image $x_T$ becomes pure Gaussian noise, completely losing its original information. Mathematically, $q(x_t | x_{t-1}) = \mathcal{N}(x_t; \sqrt{1-\beta_t}x_{t-1}, \beta_t I)$, where $\beta_t$ is a variance schedule. A key property is that $q(x_t | x_0)$ can be sampled directly, meaning $x_t = \sqrt{\bar{\alpha}_t}x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar{\alpha}_t}\epsilon$, where $\bar{\alpha}_t = \prod_{s=1}^t (1-\beta_s)$ and $\epsilon \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I)$.
        *   **Reverse (Denoising) Process:** This is a learned Markov chain that attempts to reverse the forward process, starting from pure noise $x_T$ and gradually removing noise to reconstruct the original image $x_0$. A neural network (typically a U-Net) is trained to predict the noise $\epsilon_t$ added at each step $t$, or directly predict $x_0$. The model learns the conditional probability $p_\theta(x_{t-1} | x_t)$, which is approximated as a Gaussian distribution. By iteratively sampling from this learned distribution, the model transforms random noise into a meaningful image.
        *   **Relationship & Purpose:** The forward process provides a clear, tractable way to generate noisy versions of images, which serves as the training data for the reverse process. The reverse process then learns to invert this transformation, effectively learning to generate realistic data by iteratively denoising.

2.  **Question:** What is "Classifier-Free Guidance" in the context of diffusion models, and why is it a significant improvement for conditional image generation?
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:** Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) is a technique used in conditional diffusion models to improve the alignment between generated images and their conditioning input (e.g., text prompts). Instead of relying on a separate classifier model to guide the diffusion process (as in classifier guidance), CFG achieves this by training a single diffusion model that can operate both conditionally (e.g., with a text prompt `c`) and unconditionally (e.g., with an empty or null prompt `∅`).
        During inference, the model performs two forward passes for each denoising step: one with the actual prompt `c` and one with the null prompt `∅`. The predicted noise $\epsilon_\theta(x_t, c)$ and $\epsilon_\theta(x_t, \emptyset)$ are then combined to produce a new, "guided" noise prediction:
        $\hat{\epsilon} = \epsilon_\theta(x_t, \emptyset) + w \cdot (\epsilon_\theta(x_t, c) - \epsilon_\theta(x_t, \emptyset))$
        where `w` is the guidance scale. A higher `w` pushes the generation more strongly towards the prompt, often at the cost of diversity or quality if too high.
        CFG is significant because it:
        *   **Simplifies Training:** Eliminates the need for a separate, pre-trained classifier, reducing complexity and potential for mismatch between the diffusion model and the classifier.
        *   **Improves Quality:** Often leads to higher quality and more prompt-aligned generations compared to classifier guidance or unguided generation.
        *   **Flexibility:** Allows dynamic control over the trade-off between prompt adherence and sample diversity during inference simply by adjusting the guidance scale `w`.

3.  **Question:** Differentiate between a "Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM)" and a "Latent Diffusion Model (LDM)" like Stable Diffusion. What is the primary advantage of LDMs?
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        *   **DDPM (Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model):** Operates directly in the pixel space of images. The diffusion process adds noise to and denoises the raw pixel values of an image. This means the U-Net in a DDPM processes high-dimensional pixel data at every step, making it computationally expensive, especially for high-resolution images.
        *   **LDM (Latent Diffusion Model):** Operates in a compressed, lower-dimensional latent space rather than the high-dimensional pixel space. An autoencoder (composed of an encoder and a decoder) is used: the encoder maps images from pixel space to latent space, and the decoder maps latent representations back to pixel space. The diffusion process (noising and denoising) occurs entirely within this latent space.
        *   **Primary Advantage of LDMs:** The primary advantage of LDMs is **computational efficiency**. By performing the diffusion process in a lower-dimensional latent space, the U-Net operates on significantly smaller tensors. This dramatically reduces the computational cost and memory requirements for both training and inference, enabling the generation of high-resolution images much faster and with fewer resources than pixel-space DDPMs. This efficiency is what made models like Stable Diffusion practical for widespread use.

4.  **Question:** Explain the concept of "negative prompting" in the context of Stable Diffusion and how it improves image generation. Provide an example.
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:** Negative prompting is a technique used in text-to-image diffusion models, particularly Stable Diffusion, to guide the generation process *away* from certain undesirable attributes or elements. While a positive prompt tells the model what to include, a negative prompt tells it what to *exclude* or *avoid*.
        It works by influencing the Classifier-Free Guidance mechanism. When a negative prompt is provided, the model effectively calculates the noise prediction for the positive prompt, the null prompt, and the negative prompt. The guidance then pushes the generation towards the positive prompt while simultaneously pushing it away from the negative prompt.
        **Improvement:** Negative prompting significantly improves image generation by allowing users to:
        *   **Reduce Artifacts:** Avoid common generation issues like distorted limbs, extra fingers, blurry images, or watermarks.
        *   **Refine Composition:** Remove unwanted background elements, specific colors, or stylistic choices.
        *   **Increase Control:** Gain finer control over the output, ensuring the image aligns more closely with the user's vision by explicitly stating what *not* to include.
        **Example:**
        *   **Positive Prompt:** `A majestic lion standing on a savannah at sunset, golden hour, highly detailed, photorealistic.`
        *   **Negative Prompt:** `blurry, low quality, distorted, extra limbs, watermark, text, cartoon, ugly, bad anatomy, deformed.`
        This negative prompt would help ensure the generated lion is sharp, realistic, and free from common generative AI imperfections.

### Section 2: Code Tracing & Interpretation (3 Questions)

5.  **Question:** Consider the following simplified Python code snippet representing a single denoising step in a diffusion model. Assume `model` is a pre-trained U-Net, `scheduler` handles the noise schedule, and `latent` is the current noisy latent representation.
    ```python
    import torch

    def denoising_step(model, scheduler, latent, timestep, text_embeddings, guidance_scale):
        # 1. Expand the latents if we are doing classifier-free guidance.
        #    For simplicity, assume text_embeddings already contains both conditional and unconditional.
        latent_model_input = torch.cat([latent] * 2) # Assume batch size 1, so expand to 2 for cond/uncond
        latent_model_input = scheduler.scale_model_input(latent_model_input, timestep)

        # 2. Predict the noise residual
        noise_pred = model(latent_model_input, timestep, encoder_hidden_states=text_embeddings).sample

        # 3. Perform classifier-free guidance
        noise_pred_uncond, noise_pred_text = noise_pred.chunk(2)
        noise_pred = noise_pred_uncond + guidance_scale * (noise_pred_text - noise_pred_uncond)

        # 4. Compute the previous noisy sample x_t -> x_t-1
        latent = scheduler.step(noise_pred, timestep, latent).prev_sample
        return latent
    ```
    If `guidance_scale` is set to `0.0`, describe the behavior of the `noise_pred` calculation (step 3) and its implications for the generated image. What if `guidance_scale` is very high (e.g., `20.0`)?
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        *   **If `guidance_scale` is `0.0`:**
            The calculation for `noise_pred` becomes:
            `noise_pred = noise_pred_uncond + 0.0 * (noise_pred_text - noise_pred_uncond)`
            `noise_pred = noise_pred_uncond`
            This means the model will effectively ignore the conditional text prompt (`text_embeddings` used for `noise_pred_text`) and only use the unconditional noise prediction (`noise_pred_uncond`) to guide the denoising process. The generated image will be largely unguided by the prompt, resulting in diverse but potentially irrelevant outputs, similar to generating images from pure noise without any specific instruction.
        *   **If `guidance_scale` is very high (e.g., `20.0`):**
            A very high `guidance_scale` amplifies the difference between the conditional and unconditional noise predictions. The term `(noise_pred_text - noise_pred_uncond)` represents the "direction" in which the model should move to align with the text prompt. Multiplying this by a large `guidance_scale` pushes the generation very strongly towards the prompt's semantics.
            **Implications:**
            *   **Strong Prompt Adherence:** The generated images will adhere very strictly to the text prompt, often producing outputs that are highly specific and detailed according to the prompt.
            *   **Reduced Diversity:** The diversity of generated images will likely decrease, as the model is heavily constrained by the prompt.
            *   **Potential for Artifacts/Oversaturation:** Extremely high guidance scales can sometimes lead to undesirable artifacts, over-saturation, or unnatural-looking images because the model is forced too aggressively into a particular direction, potentially moving out of the natural data manifold. It can also lead to "prompt leakage" where the model generates elements that are technically in the prompt but don't look natural.

6.  **Question:** You are fine-tuning a Stable Diffusion model using the LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) technique. After training for several epochs, you observe that your generated images, while showing some elements of your target style, also exhibit significant "overfitting" – they look too similar to specific images in your training dataset, or they show repetitive patterns. Which of the following parameters would you primarily adjust to mitigate this overfitting, and why?
    *   A) Increase the learning rate.
    *   B) Decrease the LoRA rank (`r`).
    *   C) Increase the number of training steps/epochs.
    *   D) Decrease the LoRA alpha (`alpha`).
    *   E) Implement data augmentation.

    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        The primary adjustments to mitigate overfitting in this scenario would be **B) Decrease the LoRA rank (`r`)** and **E) Implement data augmentation**.
        *   **B) Decrease the LoRA rank (`r`):** LoRA rank `r` controls the expressiveness of the low-rank matrices added to the model. A higher rank allows for more complex adaptations, which can lead to overfitting if the dataset is small or the training is too long. Decreasing `r` reduces the number of trainable parameters and thus the model's capacity to memorize specific training examples, encouraging it to learn more generalized features.
        *   **E) Implement data augmentation:** Data augmentation (e.g., random rotations, flips, crops, color jitter) artificially expands the diversity of your training dataset without collecting new images. This exposes the model to more variations, making it less likely to memorize specific training examples and improving its generalization capabilities.
        *   **Why others are less effective or counterproductive:**
            *   A) Increasing the learning rate might make the model converge faster but could also lead to instability or even more aggressive overfitting if not carefully managed.
            *   C) Increasing the number of training steps/epochs would *exacerbate* overfitting, as the model would have more opportunities to memorize the training data.
            *   D) LoRA alpha (`alpha`) scales the LoRA weights. Decreasing `alpha` would reduce the impact of the LoRA layers, potentially making the model less able to learn the desired style at all, rather than just preventing overfitting. It's more about strength of adaptation than preventing overfitting directly.

7.  **Question:** You are using the `diffusers` library in Python to generate an image. You notice that the generated images are often blurry or lack fine details. You check your `scheduler` configuration and see that you are using `DDIMScheduler` with `num_inference_steps=20`. What change would you make to the `scheduler` configuration to potentially improve the detail and sharpness of the generated images, and why?
    ```python
    from diffusers import StableDiffusionPipeline, DDIMScheduler
    import torch

    pipe = StableDiffusionPipeline.from_pretrained("runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5", torch_dtype=torch.float16)
    pipe.scheduler = DDIMScheduler.from_config(pipe.scheduler.config)
    pipe.scheduler.set_timesteps(20) # num_inference_steps is set here

    prompt = "a photo of an astronaut riding a horse on mars"
    image = pipe(prompt).images[0]
    ```
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        To potentially improve the detail and sharpness of the generated images, you should **increase the `num_inference_steps` parameter** for the `DDIMScheduler`.
        **Reasoning:**
        The `num_inference_steps` parameter determines how many steps the denoising process takes to go from pure noise to a generated image.
        *   **Fewer steps (e.g., 20):** While faster, fewer steps mean the model has less opportunity to refine the image and remove subtle noise. Each step takes a larger "jump" in the denoising process, which can lead to approximations that manifest as blurriness or lack of fine detail.
        *   **More steps (e.g., 50, 100, or even 150):** More steps allow the model to take smaller, more precise denoising steps. This provides more opportunities for the U-Net to accurately predict and remove noise, leading to a more refined, detailed, and sharper final image. There's a diminishing return, but increasing from 20 to 50 or 75 is often a good balance between speed and quality.
        **Example Code Change:**
        ```python
        # ... (previous code)
        pipe.scheduler.set_timesteps(75) # Increased from 20 to 75
        # ... (rest of the code)
        ```

### Section 3: Code Writing & Implementation (4 Questions)

8.  **Question:** Write a Python function using the `diffusers` library that takes a text prompt, a negative prompt, and a `guidance_scale` as input, and generates an image using a pre-trained Stable Diffusion pipeline. Ensure the function handles the negative prompt correctly.
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        ```python
        from diffusers import StableDiffusionPipeline
        import torch

        def generate_image_with_negative_prompt(
            prompt: str,
            negative_prompt: str,
            guidance_scale: float = 7.5,
            model_id: str = "runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5",
            num_inference_steps: int = 50,
            seed: int = 42
        ) -> torch.Tensor:
            """
            Generates an image using Stable Diffusion with a positive and negative prompt.

            Args:
                prompt (str): The positive text prompt for image generation.
                negative_prompt (str): The negative text prompt to guide generation away from.
                guidance_scale (float): Classifier-free guidance scale. Higher values mean more adherence to prompt.
                model_id (str): The ID of the pre-trained Stable Diffusion model to use.
                num_inference_steps (int): Number of denoising steps.
                seed (int): Random seed for reproducibility.

            Returns:
                torch.Tensor: The generated image as a PIL Image object.
            """
            # Load the pipeline
            # Using float16 for memory efficiency, especially on GPUs
            pipe = StableDiffusionPipeline.from_pretrained(model_id, torch_dtype=torch.float16)
            pipe.to("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")

            # Set a generator for reproducibility
            generator = torch.Generator("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu").manual_seed(seed)

            # Generate the image
            output = pipe(
                prompt=prompt,
                negative_prompt=negative_prompt,
                guidance_scale=guidance_scale,
                num_inference_steps=num_inference_steps,
                generator=generator
            )

            return output.images[0] # Return the first (and only) generated image

        # Example usage:
        if __name__ == "__main__":
            positive_prompt = "a vibrant watercolor painting of a whimsical forest, magical, detailed"
            negative_prompt = "blurry, low quality, dark, gloomy, ugly, text, watermark"
            generated_img = generate_image_with_negative_prompt(positive_prompt, negative_prompt, guidance_scale=8.0)
            generated_img.save("generated_watercolor_forest.png")
            print("Image generated and saved as generated_watercolor_forest.png")

            # Example with a different prompt and higher guidance
            positive_prompt_2 = "a futuristic city at night, neon lights, cyberpunk style, rain, reflections"
            negative_prompt_2 = "daylight, cartoon, simple, empty, low resolution"
            generated_img_2 = generate_image_with_negative_prompt(positive_prompt_2, negative_prompt_2, guidance_scale=10.0, seed=123)
            generated_img_2.save("generated_cyberpunk_city.png")
            print("Image generated and saved as generated_cyberpunk_city.png")
        ```
        *   **Partial Credit Guidance:** Full credit requires correct use of `StableDiffusionPipeline`, `prompt`, `negative_prompt`, and `guidance_scale` parameters. Partial credit for correct pipeline loading but incorrect parameter passing, or for missing `torch_dtype` for efficiency.

9.  **Question:** Imagine you are preparing a dataset for fine-tuning a diffusion model. You have a folder `my_dataset/` containing images. Write a Python snippet using `Pillow` and `torchvision.transforms` to:
    1.  Load an image from a given path.
    2.  Resize it to 512x512 pixels.
    3.  Randomly horizontally flip it with a probability of 0.5.
    4.  Convert it to a PyTorch tensor and normalize its pixel values to the range `[-1, 1]` (a common input range for diffusion models).
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        ```python
        from PIL import Image
        import torchvision.transforms as transforms
        import torch
        import os

        def preprocess_image_for_diffusion(image_path: str) -> torch.Tensor:
            """
            Loads, transforms, and normalizes an image for diffusion model input.

            Args:
                image_path (str): The file path to the image.

            Returns:
                torch.Tensor: The processed image tensor in the range [-1, 1].
            """
            # Define the transformations
            preprocess = transforms.Compose([
                transforms.Resize((512, 512)),  # Resize to 512x512
                transforms.RandomHorizontalFlip(p=0.5), # Randomly flip horizontally
                transforms.ToTensor(),          # Convert to PyTorch tensor (0-1 range)
                transforms.Normalize(mean=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5], std=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5]) # Normalize to [-1, 1]
            ])

            # Load the image
            try:
                image = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
            except FileNotFoundError:
                print(f"Error: Image not found at {image_path}")
                return None
            except Exception as e:
                print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
                return None

            # Apply transformations
            processed_image = preprocess(image)
            return processed_image

        # Example usage (assuming you have a dummy image 'test_image.jpg'):
        if __name__ == "__main__":
            # Create a dummy image for testing
            dummy_image_path = "test_image.jpg"
            if not os.path.exists(dummy_image_path):
                dummy_img = Image.new('RGB', (800, 600), color = 'red')
                dummy_img.save(dummy_image_path)
                print(f"Created dummy image: {dummy_image_path}")

            processed_tensor = preprocess_image_for_diffusion(dummy_image_path)

            if processed_tensor is not None:
                print(f"Processed tensor shape: {processed_tensor.shape}")
                print(f"Min pixel value: {processed_tensor.min().item()}")
                print(f"Max pixel value: {processed_tensor.max().item()}")
                # Expected output: shape torch.Size([3, 512, 512]), min approx -1.0, max approx 1.0
        ```
        *   **Partial Credit Guidance:** Full credit requires all four steps correctly implemented. Partial credit for correct resizing and tensor conversion but incorrect normalization or missing horizontal flip.

10. **Question:** You're working with a custom dataset for fine-tuning a Stable Diffusion model. Each data point consists of an image and a corresponding text caption. Write a simple PyTorch `Dataset` class that loads images and tokenizes their captions using a `CLIPTokenizer`. Assume images are in a folder `images/` and captions are in a CSV file `captions.csv` with columns `image_filename` and `caption`.
    ```csv
    image_filename,caption
    img_001.jpg,A dog playing in the park
    img_002.png,A cat sleeping on a sofa
    # ...
    ```
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        ```python
        import torch
        from torch.utils.data import Dataset
        from PIL import Image
        import pandas as pd
        import os
        from transformers import CLIPTokenizer
        import torchvision.transforms as transforms

        class CustomDiffusionDataset(Dataset):
            def __init__(self, captions_csv_path: str, images_dir: str, tokenizer: CLIPTokenizer,
                         image_size: int = 512, max_token_length: int = 77):
                """
                Initializes the dataset for diffusion model fine-tuning.

                Args:
                    captions_csv_path (str): Path to the CSV file containing image filenames and captions.
                    images_dir (str): Directory where image files are stored.
                    tokenizer (CLIPTokenizer): Pre-trained CLIP tokenizer for text processing.
                    image_size (int): Target size for image resizing (e.g., 512).
                    max_token_length (int): Maximum length for tokenized captions.
                """
                self.captions_df = pd.read_csv(captions_csv_path)
                self.images_dir = images_dir
                self.tokenizer = tokenizer
                self.max_token_length = max_token_length

                self.transform = transforms.Compose([
                    transforms.Resize((image_size, image_size), interpolation=transforms.InterpolationMode.BILINEAR),
                    transforms.RandomHorizontalFlip(),
                    transforms.ToTensor(),
                    transforms.Normalize([0.5], [0.5]), # Normalize to [-1, 1]
                ])

            def __len__(self):
                return len(self.captions_df)

            def __getitem__(self, idx):
                row = self.captions_df.iloc[idx]
                image_filename = row['image_filename']
                caption = str(row['caption']) # Ensure caption is string

                # Load and transform image
                image_path = os.path.join(self.images_dir, image_filename)
                try:
                    image = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
                    image = self.transform(image)
                except Exception as e:
                    print(f"Warning: Could not load or process image {image_path}. Error: {e}")
                    # Return a dummy tensor or handle error appropriately for training
                    # For simplicity, returning a zero tensor here, but a more robust
                    # solution might skip this item or replace it.
                    image = torch.zeros(3, self.transform.transforms[0].size[0], self.transform.transforms[0].size[1])

                # Tokenize caption
                text_inputs = self.tokenizer(
                    caption,
                    max_length=self.max_token_length,
                    padding="max_length",
                    truncation=True,
                    return_tensors="pt"
                )
                input_ids = text_inputs.input_ids.squeeze() # Remove batch dimension

                return {
                    "pixel_values": image,
                    "input_ids": input_ids
                }

        # Example Usage:
        if __name__ == "__main__":
            # Create dummy files for demonstration
            os.makedirs("dummy_images", exist_ok=True)
            Image.new('RGB', (100, 100), color = 'red').save("dummy_images/img_001.jpg")
            Image.new('RGB', (100, 100), color = 'blue').save("dummy_images/img_002.png")
            Image.new('RGB', (100, 100), color = 'green').save("dummy_images/img_003.jpeg")

            dummy_captions_data = {
                'image_filename': ['img_001.jpg', 'img_002.png', 'img_003.jpeg'],
                'caption': ['A red square', 'A blue circle', 'A green triangle']
            }
            dummy_df = pd.DataFrame(dummy_captions_data)
            dummy_df.to_csv("dummy_captions.csv", index=False)

            # Initialize tokenizer
            tokenizer = CLIPTokenizer.from_pretrained("openai/clip-vit-base-patch32")

            # Create dataset instance
            dataset = CustomDiffusionDataset(
                captions_csv_path="dummy_captions.csv",
                images_dir="dummy_images",
                tokenizer=tokenizer
            )

            print(f"Dataset length: {len(dataset)}")

            # Access a sample
            sample = dataset[0]
            print(f"Sample image tensor shape: {sample['pixel_values'].shape}")
            print(f"Sample input_ids shape: {sample['input_ids'].shape}")
            print(f"Sample input_ids (tokenized caption): {sample['input_ids']}")
            print(f"Decoded caption: {tokenizer.decode(sample['input_ids'], skip_special_tokens=True)}")

            # Clean up dummy files
            os.remove("dummy_captions.csv")
            os.remove("dummy_images/img_001.jpg")
            os.remove("dummy_images/img_002.png")
            os.remove("dummy_images/img_003.jpeg")
            os.rmdir("dummy_images")
        ```
        *   **Partial Credit Guidance:** Full credit for correctly implementing `__len__`, `__getitem__`, loading images with `PIL`, applying transforms, and tokenizing captions with `CLIPTokenizer`. Partial credit for correct structure but issues with image loading/transforms or tokenizer usage.

11. **Question:** You want to perform a simple image-to-image translation using a pre-trained Stable Diffusion pipeline and a ControlNet model. Write a Python snippet that loads a ControlNet model (e.g., Canny edge detection) and integrates it with a Stable Diffusion pipeline to generate an image from an input image's Canny edges and a text prompt. Assume you have an input image `input_image.png` and a function `get_canny_edges(image_path)` that returns a PIL image of its Canny edges.
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        ```python
        from diffusers import StableDiffusionControlNetPipeline, ControlNetModel, UniPCMultistepScheduler
        from diffusers.utils import load_image
        import torch
        from PIL import Image
        import cv2
        import numpy as np
        import os

        # Dummy function to simulate Canny edge detection
        def get_canny_edges(image_path: str) -> Image.Image:
            """
            Loads an image, converts it to grayscale, applies Canny edge detection,
            and returns the result as a PIL Image.
            """
            image = cv2.imread(image_path)
            if image is None:
                raise FileNotFoundError(f"Image not found at {image_path}")
            image = cv2.cvtColor(image, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
            image = np.array(image)

            low_threshold = 100
            high_threshold = 200

            image = cv2.Canny(image, low_threshold, high_threshold)
            image = image[:, :, None]
            image = np.concatenate([image, image, image], axis=2)
            canny_image = Image.fromarray(image)
            return canny_image

        def generate_image_with_controlnet(
            input_image_path: str,
            prompt: str,
            controlnet_conditioning_scale: float = 1.0,
            seed: int = 42
        ) -> Image.Image:
            """
            Generates an image using Stable Diffusion with ControlNet (Canny edges).

            Args:
                input_image_path (str): Path to the input image for Canny edge detection.
                prompt (str): The text prompt for image generation.
                controlnet_conditioning_scale (float): Scale for ControlNet guidance.
                seed (int): Random seed for reproducibility.

            Returns:
                PIL.Image.Image: The generated image.
            """
            # 1. Load ControlNet model
            controlnet = ControlNetModel.from_pretrained(
                "lllyasviel/sd-controlnet-canny", torch_dtype=torch.float16
            )

            # 2. Load Stable Diffusion pipeline with ControlNet
            pipe = StableDiffusionControlNetPipeline.from_pretrained(
                "runwayml/stable-diffusion-v1-5", controlnet=controlnet, torch_dtype=torch.float16
            )
            pipe.scheduler = UniPCMultistepScheduler.from_config(pipe.scheduler.config)
            pipe.to("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")

            # 3. Get Canny edges from the input image
            control_image = get_canny_edges(input_image_path)
            # control_image.save("canny_output.png") # Optional: save Canny output for inspection

            # 4. Set generator for reproducibility
            generator = torch.Generator("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu").manual_seed(seed)

            # 5. Generate the image
            output = pipe(
                prompt,
                control_image=control_image,
                num_inference_steps=20, # Can increase for better quality
                generator=generator,
                controlnet_conditioning_scale=controlnet_conditioning_scale,
            )

            return output.images[0]

        # Example Usage:
        if __name__ == "__main__":
            # Create a dummy input image for testing
            dummy_input_image_path = "input_image.png"
            if not os.path.exists(dummy_input_image_path):
                # Create a simple image with a distinct shape for Canny to detect
                img = Image.new('RGB', (512, 512), color = 'white')
                from PIL import ImageDraw
                draw = ImageDraw.Draw(img)
                draw.ellipse((100, 100, 400, 400), fill='black', outline='black')
                img.save(dummy_input_image_path)
                print(f"Created dummy input image: {dummy_input_image_path}")

            prompt_text = "a futuristic robot, highly detailed, metallic, sci-fi art"
            generated_img = generate_image_with_controlnet(
                dummy_input_image_path,
                prompt_text,
                controlnet_conditioning_scale=0.8
            )
            generated_img.save("controlnet_robot_output.png")
            print("ControlNet image generated and saved as controlnet_robot_output.png")

            # Clean up dummy files
            os.remove(dummy_input_image_path)
        ```
        *   **Partial Credit Guidance:** Full credit for correctly loading `ControlNetModel` and `StableDiffusionControlNetPipeline`, passing `control_image` and `controlnet_conditioning_scale` to the pipeline. Partial credit for correct pipeline loading but issues with ControlNet integration or parameter passing.

### Section 4: Design & Debugging Problems (4 Questions)

12. **Question:** You are fine-tuning a Stable Diffusion model on a dataset of 100 high-resolution (1024x1024) images of architectural designs. After a few epochs, you notice that your GPU runs out of memory (`CUDA out of memory` error) during training. Propose at least three distinct strategies to address this memory issue, explaining how each strategy helps.
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        Here are three distinct strategies to address `CUDA out of memory` during fine-tuning:
        1.  **Reduce Batch Size:** The most straightforward approach. Each batch of images requires memory for its forward pass, gradients, and intermediate activations. By reducing the `batch_size` (e.g., from 4 to 2 or 1), you decrease the total memory footprint per iteration. This is a common first step, though it might increase training time and potentially affect gradient stability (which can be counteracted by increasing `gradient_accumulation_steps`).
        2.  **Gradient Accumulation:** Instead of processing a full `effective_batch_size` in one go, you can process smaller `micro_batches` and accumulate their gradients over several steps before performing a single optimization update. For example, with `gradient_accumulation_steps=4` and `batch_size=1`, you achieve an effective batch size of 4 while only needing memory for a single image's forward/backward pass at any given moment. This effectively trades computation time for memory.
        3.  **Leverage Mixed Precision Training (e.g., `torch.float16` or `bfloat16`):** Modern GPUs (especially NVIDIA Volta, Ampere, and newer architectures) can perform computations much faster and with less memory using half-precision floating-point numbers (FP16 or BF16). By converting model weights and activations to `torch.float16`, you effectively halve the memory usage for these tensors. PyTorch's `torch.cuda.amp` (Automatic Mixed Precision) makes this relatively easy to implement, handling the necessary casting and maintaining numerical stability.
        4.  **Reduce Image Resolution:** While your target is 1024x1024, training at a slightly lower resolution (e.g., 768x768 or even 512x512) for initial epochs or for the entire fine-tuning process can significantly reduce memory usage, as memory scales quadratically with image dimensions. You can then potentially upscale or use an image super-resolution model post-generation if higher resolution is critical for inference.
        5.  **Offload to CPU (CPU Offloading/DeepSpeed ZeRO/bitsandbytes):** More advanced techniques involve offloading parts of the model (e.g., optimizer states, gradients, or even model parameters) to CPU memory and bringing them back to the GPU only when needed. Libraries like DeepSpeed (with ZeRO optimization stages) or `bitsandbytes` (for 8-bit or 4-bit quantization) can manage this automatically, allowing much larger models to fit into limited GPU memory.

13. **Question:** Your fine-tuned Stable Diffusion model is generating images that consistently have a strong color cast (e.g., everything looks too blue or too yellow), despite the input images in your training dataset having balanced colors. What could be two potential causes for this issue, and how would you debug or fix each?
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        Here are two potential causes and their solutions:
        1.  **Cause: Data Preprocessing Mismatch/Error:**
            *   **Explanation:** The most common cause for a consistent color cast is an issue with how the images were preprocessed during training. This could be incorrect normalization, a color space conversion error, or even a subtle bug in how the training data loader handles image channels. For example, if you normalized using `mean=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5], std=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5]` but your images were actually in a different range or had a different underlying distribution, the model might learn to compensate by shifting colors. Or, if `PIL.Image` loaded images as `RGBA` and you didn't explicitly convert to `RGB` before `ToTensor`, the alpha channel might interfere.
            *   **Debugging/Fixing:**
                *   **Inspect Training Data:** Load a few batches from your `DataLoader` *after* all transformations and visualize them. Use `matplotlib` to plot the images and check their color balance. Also, calculate the mean and standard deviation of pixel values across your *entire processed training dataset* to ensure they align with the normalization values used (e.g., if you normalized to `[-1, 1]`, the mean should be close to 0).
                *   **Verify Normalization:** Double-check the `mean` and `std` values in your `transforms.Normalize` call. Ensure they are appropriate for the range of your images *after* `ToTensor()` (which typically converts to `[0, 1]`). If you're normalizing to `[-1, 1]`, `mean=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5]` and `std=[0.5, 0.5, 0.5]` is correct.
                *   **Color Space Consistency:** Explicitly convert images to `RGB` after loading (`Image.open(path).convert("RGB")`) to avoid issues with alpha channels or other color modes.
        2.  **Cause: Insufficient Diversity or Bias in Training Data Colors:**
            *   **Explanation:** Even if individual images in your dataset have balanced colors, the *overall average* color or the dominant color palette across your entire training dataset might lean towards a specific hue. If your dataset of architectural designs predominantly features buildings under a clear blue sky, the model might learn to associate "architecture" with a bluish tint, even if the individual images are not "too blue."
            *   **Debugging/Fixing:**
                *   **Analyze Dataset Color Distribution:** Perform a statistical analysis of the color distribution (e.g., average RGB values, histogram of hues) across your entire training dataset. This can reveal subtle biases.
                *   **Augment Color:** Introduce color augmentation during training, such as `transforms.ColorJitter` (adjusting brightness, contrast, saturation, hue) or `transforms.RandomGrayscale`. This forces the model to learn features that are invariant to minor color shifts and makes it less reliant on a specific color palette.
                *   **Expand Dataset:** If possible, augment your dataset with more diverse images that cover a broader range of lighting conditions and color palettes.

14. **Question:** You've trained a custom LoRA adapter for Stable Diffusion to generate images of "fantasy creatures." When you generate images using a prompt like "a majestic dragon flying over mountains," the results are good. However, when you try "a tiny pixie sitting on a mushroom," the pixie often looks distorted or doesn't appear at all, even though "pixie" was in your training data. What could be a likely reason for this discrepancy, and how would you try to fix it?
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        **Likely Reason:** The discrepancy likely stems from a **scale bias or lack of diversity in scale within your training dataset** for the "pixie" concept, or a **weakness in the model's ability to generalize scale relationships** from the LoRA fine-tuning.
        *   **Explanation:**
            *   **Scale Bias:** Your "fantasy creatures" dataset might have predominantly featured larger creatures (like dragons) or pixies that were always depicted in a certain size relative to their surroundings. If the model only saw "pixies" in close-up shots or at a specific relative scale, it might struggle to generate them at a "tiny" scale within a broader scene. The LoRA adapter might have learned to associate "pixie" with a specific visual size, and when prompted for "tiny," it conflicts with this learned scale.
            *   **Prompt Interference:** The word "tiny" might be conflicting with the learned representation of "pixie" from the LoRA, or the base model's understanding of "tiny" might not be effectively integrated with the LoRA's "pixie" concept.
            *   **Limited LoRA Capacity:** While LoRA is powerful, it has limited capacity. If the concept of "pixie" at various scales was not sufficiently represented or diverse enough in the fine-tuning data, the LoRA might not have learned this generalization well.

        **Debugging/Fixing Strategies:**
        1.  **Augment Training Data for Scale Diversity:**
            *   **Action:** If you have control over the training data, ensure your "pixie" images include examples at various scales: close-ups, medium shots, and wide shots where the pixie appears genuinely "tiny" relative to its environment (e.g., a pixie next to a large flower, a pixie on a human's finger).
            *   **How it helps:** This teaches the LoRA adapter to understand the concept of "pixie" independent of a fixed scale, making it more robust to scale-related prompts.
        2.  **Refine Prompt Engineering with Scale Modifiers:**
            *   **Action:** Experiment with more explicit scale descriptors. Instead of just "tiny pixie," try "a miniature pixie," "a pixie, very small," or "a pixie, 1 inch tall." Also, try combining the LoRA with strong base model scale prompts like "macro shot of a pixie" or "wide shot, a tiny pixie."
            *   **How it helps:** This provides stronger guidance to the base model and LoRA, helping them to better interpret the desired scale. Sometimes, the base model's understanding of scale needs to be explicitly leveraged.
        3.  **Adjust LoRA Training Parameters:**
            *   **Action:** If the issue persists, consider re-training the LoRA with a slightly higher `learning_rate` or a longer training schedule, especially if the original training was too short to fully capture complex concepts like variable scale. You might also experiment with a slightly higher LoRA rank (`r`) if you suspect the model didn't have enough capacity to learn the nuance.
            *   **How it helps:** A more thoroughly trained LoRA might better integrate the "pixie" concept with the base model's understanding of scale.
        4.  **Isolate the LoRA Effect:**
            *   **Action:** Generate images with the base model *without* the LoRA using the "tiny pixie" prompt. Compare these to the LoRA-generated images. If the base model *also* struggles, it indicates a fundamental challenge with the prompt or the base model's understanding of "tiny" combined with "pixie." If the base model does better, it points to the LoRA's specific learning.
            *   **How it helps:** Helps diagnose whether the issue lies specifically with the LoRA's learned representation or a broader challenge with the prompt.

15. **Question:** You are developing a generative AI application for a client. The client wants to ensure that the generated images are not only high quality but also ethically sound, avoiding harmful biases or inappropriate content. What are two key ethical considerations you would prioritize in your development and deployment pipeline for a diffusion model, and what practical steps would you take for each?
    *   **Correct Answer/Explanation:**
        Here are two key ethical considerations and practical steps:

        1.  **Ethical Consideration: Bias and Fair Representation:**
            *   **Explanation:** Diffusion models, like all machine learning models, learn from their training data. If the training data contains biases (e.g., under-representing certain demographics, over-representing stereotypes, or showing a narrow range of professions for specific groups), the generative model will amplify these biases. This can lead to outputs that are stereotypical, exclusionary, or even perpetuate harmful representations (e.g., generating only male doctors or only female nurses, or associating certain ethnicities with specific negative contexts).
            *   **Practical Steps:**
                *   **Dataset Auditing & Curation:** Before training or fine-tuning, thoroughly audit the training dataset for demographic, cultural, and social biases. Use tools to analyze the diversity of subjects, skin tones, genders, occupations, and cultural contexts. Actively curate or augment the dataset to ensure a more balanced and representative distribution, or at least be aware of the biases that remain.
                *   **Bias Mitigation Techniques:** During training, explore techniques like re-weighting biased samples, using adversarial debiasing methods, or employing specific regularization to encourage more diverse outputs.
                *   **Output Monitoring & Evaluation:** Implement systematic evaluation metrics beyond just image quality. Develop a set of "bias tests" where you input diverse prompts (e.g., "a CEO," "a doctor," "a family") and analyze the generated outputs for stereotypical representations across different demographic groups. This could involve human review or automated tools for detecting sensitive attributes.
                *   **Transparency & Disclosure:** Clearly document any known biases in the model and its limitations to users and stakeholders.

        2.  **Ethical Consideration: Misinformation, Deepfakes, and Harmful Content Generation:**
            *   **Explanation:** Diffusion models are incredibly powerful at generating realistic images, which raises concerns about their potential misuse for creating convincing deepfakes, spreading misinformation, generating non-consensual intimate imagery, or producing other forms of harmful or illegal content. Even if not intentionally malicious, an unconstrained model can generate content that is inappropriate or offensive.
            *   **Practical Steps:**
                *   **Content Moderation & Filtering:** Implement robust content filters at both the input (prompt) and output (generated image) stages. This involves:
                    *   **Prompt Filtering:** Use natural language processing (NLP) models to detect and block prompts that contain hate speech, sexually explicit language, calls for violence, or other prohibited content.
                    *   **Output Filtering:** Employ image classification models or human review to automatically flag and prevent the display of generated images that violate ethical guidelines or legal standards.
                *   **Watermarking & Provenance:** Explore techniques like digital watermarking or metadata embedding to indicate that an image is AI-generated. This helps distinguish synthetic content from real content and can aid in combating misinformation.
                *   **Safety Fine-tuning:** Fine-tune the diffusion model itself on a dataset specifically designed to teach it to avoid generating harmful content, or to generate "safe" alternatives when a problematic prompt is detected. This can involve negative prompting during training or using safety classifiers to guide the denoising process away from unsafe outputs.
                *   **Access Control & Responsible Deployment:** Consider restricting access to highly powerful or unmoderated models, or deploying them with strict usage policies and monitoring mechanisms. For public-facing applications, ensure clear terms of service and reporting mechanisms for misuse.

Congratulations on completing the Cohortia course, "Generative AI for Images: Diffusion Models"! You have embarked on an exciting journey into the cutting-edge world of image synthesis, mastering the principles and practical applications of diffusion models.
